## AI Research

### [AI #191: The Hackening Begins](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZoMJdvq9meE4shyzJ/ai-191-the-hackening-begins)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>It was the probably worst day in cybersecurity history. So far. Not only is there a critical flaw in PHP that due to a leak was announced a day before the fix would be available, sending most of the internet scrambling for solutions and likely dooming a lot of long tail websites permanently, there were several other unrelated incidents. The floodgates seem to have been opened. <a href="https://manifold.markets/ZviMowshowitz/will-the-worst-day-in-cyber-securit">I have a market on whether there will be an even worse day before the end of the year</a>.</p>
<p>Another key leak this week was <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/leaked-anthropic-draft-ai-regulatory?r=67wny"><strong>the draft bill Anthropic had been shopping around Congress</strong></a>, similar to but weaker than the FRONTIER Act. <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/anthropic-does-not-understand-why?r=67wny"><strong>Anthropic’s initial attempts to explain itself</strong></a> only made the situation seem worse, as they seemed to not understand the problem with what they had done. Dario’s later response was better, but the situation remains troubling. There are enough different Anthropic developments, that I plan to have a post soon covering them all. It is a mix of news running from excellent to terrible.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25715"></span>


</div>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/i-can-see-everything?r=67wny"><strong>Will interpretability save us</strong></a>? There is a new claim that neural networks are cleaner than we thought, meaning that individual neurons mostly have a single interpretable meaning, and it is tractable to go through and label them one by one. Initial reactions got out of hand, treating this as a far bigger development than it was. This is still excellent news that makes our estimate of difficulty substantially lower going forward. The work can begin.</p>
<p>We had better get on that, because <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/misaligned?r=67wny"><strong>there have been quite a few misalignment incidents</strong></a>.</p>
<p>I continue to owe a write-up of the Gemini 4 Argon Safety Card. That is in progress and hopefully I will finish it soon. Life has gotten in the way. I may or may not have a full capabilities report on Gemini 4 Argon once I tally up the reactions. Muse Wonder definitely will not have such a post, as it has proven to have been oversold.</p>

<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/oscredwin/status/2111193911934824891">Automate proving to your incompetent building that yes, you have paid the rent</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/ecdsa-curve-origins">Find the first use of ECDSA</a>. <a href="https://x.com/choblin29/status/2111189885168112017">Claude found the seeds</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sfgaliani/status/2112189550369853856?s=61">AI models confirmed good at econometric coding in particular</a>.</p>
<p>58% of Anthropic staff expect Claude to make a Nobel prize level scientific discovery by 2027. <a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2111215889094001017">I agree with Roon that this should have been over 95%</a>. The event itself might not be over 95% to happen, but it has to be a clear favorite to happen, so it is crazy for so many employees to not agree with that.</p>
<p>Tell you what you actually want to know straight up.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2111833924338946133">PoliMath</a>: Me: How long should I bake chicken wings</p>
<p>Google top result: “When I first proposed that we start a backyard coop, my husband was deeply reticent (5 pages later) Anyway, here is our recipe for honey mustard chicken wings”</p>
<p>AI: Bake at 425 for 40-50 minutes</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/alexhvnsen/status/2112952047251472821">Vibe code your fraud.gov website</a>, probably using Opus 4.8. Which is fine, so long as you do a decent job of it.</p>


<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p>However:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2111855939909263550">PoIiMath</a>: The AI was wrong.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/StefanFSchubert/status/2111476546947183051">Yes, of course the AIs will be ‘more accurate’ to the law plus underlying ground truth, but if we wanted accuracy we would not use typical juries</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZoMJdvq9meE4shyzJ/od6btftjqjpsvxyjn8qw" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robinhanson/status/2112184320811319674">Robin Hanson</a>: Whenever you put AIs in charge of something important, you have to wonder how the firms which make them will respond to various pressures from powerful parties to slant their AIs in particular directions.</p></blockquote>
<p>The point of a jury is to provide a check on the system and avoid abuses or overly strict adherence to the letter of the law, and to protect the innocent and our rights, not to be accurate. Judges are a much superior accuracy tech. Thus, an AI jury (or one AI judge) does not solve your problem. What you likely want is a human jury that has the ability to query AIs. They can override that, but have to do it on purpose.</p>
<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/10/one-reason-i-do-not-love-ai-writing-or-even-ai-assisted-writing.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=one-reason-i-do-not-love-ai-writing-or-even-ai-assisted-writing">If you read AI writing</a>, you can then talk to the author in some sense, or read their further thoughts, but not in the sense that Tyler Cowen would like.</p>

But one of my updates after joining openai is that distillation probably plays a bigger role in the explanation than I realized.</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2110825294655562048">Opus 5.5 fast mode available for everyone in the Claude Platform at $8/$40</a>. You get up to a 2.5x speedup.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2110841899838046525">OpenAI expands access to GPT-Rosalind for scientific research</a>.</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2110797830361436604">OpenAI offers us GPT-Live-1.1</a> including new voices in various languages.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/finkd/status/2110778025629347999">Muse Spark Open exists</a>, <a href="https://research.meta.ai/blog/muse-spark-open">as in a version of Muse Spark</a> <a href="https://huggingface.co/collections/meta-models/muse-spark-open">is now an open model</a>. They call it ‘the most capable US open-weight model to date.’ That seems likely to be true. It scores a 39 on Artificial Analysis. The open weight frontier for that index is 46 for MiMo-V2.6-Pro and Qwen-3.8, and MiMo is a lot cheaper to run than Muse Spark. <a href="https://x.com/natolambert/status/2110794113415008610">Nathan Lambert</a> calls this his ‘super bowl,’ says this closes the gap substantially, and points to the <a href="https://dashboard.interconnects.ai/extra#aa-open-models">Interconnects Adoption Dashboard</a> where Spark is #8. This is not a fair competition, since the Chinese can do Massive Fraudulent Distillation, whereas Meta <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9X_ViIPA-Gc">does many things but it won’t do that</a>.</p>
<p>There are some advantages to having a semi-competitive American open model. I wouldn’t say that I trust Meta, but you can use their models in places where you would not be comfortable running Chinese models, even locally.</p>
<p>Lack of upgrades: There was no major Chinese open weights release between September 21 and October 16. No DeepSeek, GLM, Kimi, MiMo or Qwen.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/natolambert/status/2111124960705904840">Nathan Lambert</a> (I asked him why no releases): Compute restrictions, I do think there’s more internal lab-govt discussions on safety in China, less organization development. No good answers, but I’ve been monitoring &amp; pondering</p></blockquote>
<p>We then got a new DeepSeek, but no version number increment, on October 17.</p>

<h4>Wonder No More</h4>


<p><a href="https://research.meta.ai/static/muse-wonder-model-card">The Muse Wonder model card is here</a>. Is it a good model, sir?</p>
<p>Meta certainly claims it is. Their pitch, although they don’t say the line, is full ‘<a href="https://x.com/finkd/status/2109689309825794187">introducing the world’s most powerful model</a>.’ The term<a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-10-12/meta-says-new-muse-model-reaches-ai-frontier-after-14-month-push"> quoted in Bloomberg is ‘Meta says new Muse model hits AI frontier</a>.’</p>
<p>They even have an explicit line and supposed test about not “benchmaxxing.”</p>

<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZoMJdvq9meE4shyzJ/vglgjzou82dip0vuybek" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2109691967676563613">Alexandr Wang</a>: MUSE WONDER <img alt="🍉" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZoMJdvq9meE4shyzJ/fzo80ma5es3jawmawi6p" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p>muse wonder is the best model in the world for personal agents and professional agents.</p>
<p>we evaluated our model against the best models in the world across agentic and coding tasks, and we think you all will like it.</p></blockquote>
<p>Some quick responses were very positive.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/intellectronica/status/2109714090558185813">Eleanor Berger</a>: Used the not full version for only a little bit and it’s definitely very good. Like previous Muse models, it’s better in written text and interaction than all other models. And it’s very fast and smart and agentic. Time to start taking Meta seriously as a frontier AI lab.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/theo/status/2109724741234028639">Theo</a> (this was before Argon was available): My current model rankings</p>
<p>S – Opus 5.5, Muse Wonder<br />
A – GPT-6.1 Sol<br />
— Gap —<br />
Everything else.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2110074499156889605">Kelsey Piper</a>: I think it’s more narrowly a coding model than the latest Claude and ChatGPT offerings. It’s markedly worse (both on benchmarks and in use) at research and writing.</p></blockquote>
<p>Then? Crickets.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheZvi/status/2112681460083708358">My reaction thread had only two responses overnight</a>, one of them asking for the safety report and the other saying it was similar to Grok 4.7.</p>
<p>Same as it ever was. Meta is making progress, but is still solidly behind the frontier.</p>
<p>Meta also reinforced its reputation for overselling and being untrustworthy. Once again, they have misrepresented a new release. Claims of things like ‘best model in the world’ for personal agents and professional agents’ are clearly false.</p>


<h4>On Your Marks</h4>


<p>Kelsey Piper on some current AI writing capability failures:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2110809584390869077">Kelsey Piper</a>: Even frontier models have poor theory of mind, they suck at having characters know something and not say it without the narration directly telling to to you anyway. This is crippling for espionage/detective stories in particular</p>
<p>They’re very mechanical when trying to convert one story to a different format, or else have bad judgment about which details need including. Salience/importance is hard for them.</p>
<p>Their characters all talk the same or else like exaggerated caricatures of themselves. They’re really bad at the kind of subtle humor that makes the infodump parts of eg Tom Clancy keep from stalling out</p>
<p>They’re bad at pacing.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EpochAIResearch/status/2111201511787344336">Epoch reconstructs videos of runs for SpireClimb</a>, their Slay the Spire benchmark, that <a href="https://t.co/mZ1pnwXe16">speeds them up enough to be watchable</a>.</p>
<p>Win rates for Ascension 0 are now very high, yes ‘top human’ level is close to 100%:</p>

<p>Is this a coincidence or is it deliberate training? The working theory is a mix of both.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/thomascygn/status/2111871340005183930">Thomas Friedel</a>: do you think OpenAI had a RL env to train Astra specifically for STS ? Performance is unbelievably high. Higher than my dedicated STS2 model.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AlephNuul/status/2112238802228126051">Benjamin Ou</a>: Benchmark creator here! Probably yeah, GPT generations have gotten ridiculously strong at this while not necessarily being as strong on easier games like EBR-bench, and other models are pretty far behind. We know the labs like the game, so all signs point to RL.</p>
<p>Not to downplay how strong they’re getting in general at strategy card games other than StS though, there’s def some generalization going on as well.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2112007736263377099">Opus 5.5 does scarily well on ChildhoodBench</a>, as in correctly predicting major outcomes for Jack based on his childhood writings.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.jhazell.co.uk/papers/HouseholdBench.pdf">HouseholdBench asks models to predict household spending behavior</a>. Models can reduce error by ~12% versus a baseline of no changes. Open weight models lag.</p>


<h4>Choose Your Fighter</h4>


<p>I continue to see many reports like this, whereas months ago people said the opposite:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SkyeSharkie/status/2111796328183538043">Utah teapot</a>: kinda weird how a week worth of gpt 500 seems to get about as much done as 5 hours of claude 200.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Get My Agent On The Line</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/SullyOmarr/status/2111232354295701986">UPS blocks AI agents</a>. <a href="https://x.com/trikcode/status/2111622265284546620">So do for example Delta</a>, JP Morgan Chase, Charles Schwab and Amazon. The group doing this by default is going to keep expanding. The agents will still be useful, but less so.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2111511653175136478">The biggest other practical barrier for agents like Dots</a>, and also many new Claude projects, is overrefusals and misclassifications that require permissions. This is a hard problem, since the other error is worse. As an example, I gave my Dot a ‘do not delete anything that does not have source control’ rule before trying anything else, and it took that to some rather extreme places. Claude auto mode asking for permission so often is actively less safe than if it asked less, because many users will quickly stop even reading the requests.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Paul__Walsh/status/2112868073526923465">If you share a Muse conversation it can get indexed and end up in the public records.</a> In general it seems fair to assume that if you share an AI conversation there is a good chance the link will get indexed, so if you’re not okay with that then don’t share it directly.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2110584005951410188">Patrick McKenzie gets first exposure to multi-agent development workflows</a>, agrees it is obviously the future and will eat infinite tokens, and other unsurprising thoughts.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2110584005951410188">Patrick McKenzie</a>: It is deeply weird to, in the middle of extremely productive work, have a long argument with a coordination process attempting to argue with a subagent.</p>
<p>The exact contents of the hour-long argument I will not recount, but at about thirty minutes in I was wondering how anything capable of writing software code could be so obtuse. About an hour in I thought “Oh I am not first ~CEO to have this thought am I.”</p></blockquote>


<h4>Deepfaketown and Botpocalypse Soon</h4>


<p>In practice deepfakes continue to not be a substantial practical problem. Politicians still care if you enable them. It is one of the easy bad things to understand.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/C_Sommerfeldt/status/2111104346993631368">Chris Sommerfeldt</a>: [NYC] Council Speaker Julie Menin tells SpaceXAI’s Gabriela Castillo Madrid she created a sexualized image of herself using Grok. She asks how that’s possible if Grok has sufficient controls.</p>
<p>“I’m happy to have our safety team look into that specific instance,” Castillo Madrid says.</p>
<p>After the hearing, Menin says she’ll “definitely” follow up with SpaceXAI about this because she wasn’t satisfied with the testimony. She also says Castillo Madrid was “lying” when she claimed she didn’t know about the firing of a SpaceXAI employee who raised security concerns.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://stanfordreview.org/stanford-r-de-uses-ai-to-race-swap-students-for-advertising/">Stanford race-swaps</a> (and gender-swaps) students in advertising, and also to slim down and alter faces. <a href="https://stanfordreview.org/in-defense-of-stanfords-ai-race-swap/">The Stanford Review’s Zayd Patel then suggested this was good</a>, because if you fabricate diversity using AI then that beats doing it in practice. That seems to have <a href="https://x.com/StanfordReview/status/2111270233755602983">led to a Title VI complaint against the paper</a>.</p>
<p>What happens if AIs can launch frivolous lawsuits? Defense is far from free.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/emollick/status/2111595040913100837">Ethan Mollick</a>: A consequential choice will be made soon by government and companies as they are increasingly flooded with agentic AI inquiries that overcome the frictions that gated access (see the graphs – it has already started!)</p>
<p>Two options: do you make services worse or better as a result?</p>
<p>Status quo isn’t an option. You can either add additional frictions (adding fees, make services worse, ban agents) or remove them (provide more services with AI help, redesign work to make access easier). <a href="https://t.co/6vpiS5Dfwb">Paper [here]</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZoMJdvq9meE4shyzJ/mu1ngoyvq0ezaxaykrqj" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Altimor/status/2111807814532485352">Flo Crivello</a>: Seeing this in my circles — several founder friends have told me they’ve started getting hit with a huge amount of frivolous lawsuits</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2111839733076676887">Dean W. Ball</a>: It’ll be interesting in particular if people feel they can self-represent when launching frivolous lawsuits. Lawyers can be punished or suffer reputational damage for truly egregious frivolity, but a non-lawyer cares less about these things.</p></blockquote>
<p>You do both. The good news is that there are ways to ‘make it worse’ that do not do that much damage to legitimate users.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/proverbs_14_23/status/2112051340499206470">The modern world already has a bunch of scammers</a> and other predatory humans, and algorithms, out there trying to get people’s money or attention. A lot of people are not equipped to deal with things even as they are now. We feared AI would supercharge all that. It took longer than expected, and so far such effects have been rare, but it will happen more often over time. It will no longer be a news story that ‘<a href="https://nypost.com/2026/10/17/world-news/brazilian-bricklayers-dream-girl-turns-out-to-be-ai-generated/?utm_medium=social&amp;utm_campaign=nypost&amp;utm_source=twitter">Bricklayer, 49, travels 1,200 miles for dream girl – only to discover she’s AI-generated</a>.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/alexolegimas/status/2112301512185598039">Pangram could attempt to make an AI that ‘writes like a human,’ but won’t</a>. That does not mean it would write like a ‘good’ human writer either way.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2112778819983802511">Yes, some people are lying scum</a> who will present AI-written stories as real in order to drum up outrage. This is a demand-driven problem. The AI makes faking the story easier, but it also makes the fake stories easier to catch. Blame the human, not the AI.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2112748858556264902">Kelsey Piper</a>: Half a million views, tons of right wing posters like this one going “when we talk about cultural compatibility it’s not some hypothetical issue”, and no one asked “is that letter entirely AI generated” (yes) and “is it in fact almost the exact letter you get if you say ‘generate a fake HOA letter telling me to stop smoking brisket’ (yes), “would a first class letter dated Saturday October 17 arrive in time to complain about it bright and early Monday morning” (no), “is the letter consistent with Arizona laws around notice periods” (no), “how many HOAs in Arizona have a nuisance provision 8.4” (5, according to ChatGPT), and “do any of them employ a Marjorie” (no).</p></blockquote>


<h4>Fun With Media Generation</h4>


<p>I continue not to understand how restaurants end up <a href="https://x.com/deadidlegames/status/2112193662230610107">using AI images of food</a> when they have real food you can photograph. Do people actually prefer it this way? I suppose many of them do.</p>


<h4>Spy Versus Spy</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2112281368256479596">Update on the OpenAI spy</a>: Yes he used his wife’s ChatGPT to plan planting the bug. There were at least two recording devices. He was in communication with a member of the Saudi royal family and there are additional clear signs that his client was the Saudi government. There’s also this:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/KevinKhadavi/status/2112291353996791991">Kevin Khadavi</a>: My investigation into the alleged OpenAI spy’s Stanford ties got 126k views, but the response seemed unusually negative. I decided to look into it: the overwhelming majority of accounts attacking my reporting are Saudi.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/ai-companies-play-cat-and-mouse-with-secret-stealing-spies-160eec6b">The good news is that OpenAI is giving its employees anti-honeypot training</a>, as in to watch out that those trying to seduce them might be spies.</p>
<p>I like to think that Anthropic employees get similar training, except they also are told not to talk about it. If not, get on that, guys. Same for the other labs too.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/unusual_whales/status/2110817233467228203">unusual_whales</a>: BREAKING: OpenAI is warning its employees to watch out for spies, especially romantic ones, per WSJ</p>
<p>The company has cautioned staff that someone trying to seduce them may likely be a “honeypot.”</p>
<p>Even strangers approaching them in everyday places could be gathering info.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/ai-companies-play-cat-and-mouse-with-secret-stealing-spies-160eec6b">Georgia Wells, Erin Woo and Meghan Bobrowsky</a> (The Wall Street Journal): One story OpenAI tells employees to illustrate the risk: To seduce a U.S. diplomat in Russia, a foreign espionage operation first sent a blond woman to his hotel room with Champagne. When he declined, the spies sent a redheaded woman, and then next a young man. The implication for OpenAI employees was this could happen to them, according to the people.</p></blockquote>
<p>I like to think that if I was a diplomat, by the time I see the second unknown hottie show up at my hotel room with Champagne I would be pretty confident it was a trap. There are many things that are much more effective on their first try. Mix it up a little.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2110834201016832382">Kelsey Piper</a>: I worry this makes things really hard for extremely attractive people who just want to seduce an OpenAI or Anthropic employee for their money.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/oscredwin/status/2110835599540449553">Andrew Rettek</a>: And they’re the real victims here.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2110835848430415953">Kelsey Piper</a>: imagine being engaged in the innocent and time-honored trad pastime of marrying the richest guy you can find and being accused of international espionage just because you are really really hot.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/aliceisplaying/status/2110852947999699169">alice</a>: i personally want to seduce them for their extremely accurate meows</p></blockquote>

<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ZackKorman/status/2110747990067667016">Zack Korman</a>: The OpenAI employee who allegedly stole over a hundred confidential documents without OpenAI noticing for months sent the documents to his number *in mainland China*</p>

<p>This is all a big problem in its own right. If you cannot protect your secrets from spies, including the Chinese, then there is no point in trying to ‘win the race.’</p>
<p>This is also indicative of other problems. If you cannot solve your employee misalignment problems, how are you going to solve AI misalignment problems?</p>


<h4>A Young Lady’s Illustrated Primer</h4>


<p>The first best way to learn with Claude is very different from ‘create practice sheets and teacher updates’ but it is a vast upgrade on similar actions without Claude.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/coldhealing/status/2110398381201998312">cold</a>: Kids these days are getting tiger parented by Claude</p>

<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<p>Is AI changing the world? <a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2112400185011933214">Freddie deBoer says no</a>, ‘<a href="https://t.co/TujyNX3NAN">human life in 2026 appears to be almost entirely similar to human life in 2021</a>.’ Jack disagrees, pointing out that all his friends and family have had their jobs transformed.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2112400185011933214">Jack</a>: Five years of AI has changed the world dramatically more than five years of electricity or five years of the combustion engine did. It’s not even close.</p></blockquote>
<p>AI so far has simultaneously changed the world more than any other technology (at least if you exclude military techs that won active wars) in its first five years. AI so far has also changed the world dramatically less than I or many others would have expected in that time, if we had been told the path of AI capabilities. Diffusion takes time and a lot of things have been bottlenecked.</p>
<p>As AI use continues to grow exponentially, expect bigger impacts, with the impact growing faster all the time, even if capabilities mostly stop improving from here.</p>
<p>Anthropic is hiring for a smaller share of junior positions and <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/employment-at-anthropic">AI agent hours outnumber employee hours six to one</a>. Human attention is a bottleneck for AI outputs and human productivity is way up. That’s the central finding of a new Anthropic report on what it is like to work there.</p>

<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/employment-at-anthropic">Anthropic</a>: At the same time, the overall employment share of technical departments within Anthropic has fallen, from 61% to 56% in the last year. As the company expands, technical hiring has continued growing in absolute terms, but intensive hiring in sales, finance, and recruiting has outpaced it.</p>
<p>… Even clerical work tightly connected to in-person interaction has grown slowly. An executive assistant, who built a personal assistant agent for scheduling and email triage, explained: “I think we’ve already moved past the point of Claude augmenting away the really entry-level admin type role,” and instead focused their work on higher-order “chief of staff” tasks.</p>
<p>These changes have already involved reallocation: employees working at the company for at least a year were 50% more likely to move across departments each month in 2026 than they were in 2025.</p></blockquote>
<p>They have a chart for ‘reading Claude as a full time job.’</p>
<p>Some think that soon either the jobs be gone or the jobs be fake.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/EigenGender/status/2110789683026534608">EigenGender</a>: There is simply no future where humans continue to do economically useful work for much longer. Your choice is at best between various flavors of “ASI let’s humans have fun and play make believe.”</p>
<p>(to be clear this is a good thing!)</p></blockquote>
<p>Opinions are very strong on whether or not this would, if it happens, be a good thing. In some ways it is very good. In others it is very bad.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/akarlin/status/2110984426553577580">Anatoly Karlin</a>: I strongly believe that everyone countersignaling against post-scarcity utopia on the basis of the “dignity” and “sanctity” of work should have do a lengthy stint as a Dhaka garments factory or African subsistence farmer. Talk is cheap from the air conditioned ivory tower.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2111119685186146597">Jack</a>: I very strongly want a world where humans continue to pursue meaningful expertise and accomplishment, but I think Karlin is right that a lot of dignity-of-work takes come from people who have the uncommon luxury of dignified work.</p></blockquote>
<p>Some view not having real work as being kind of AI pets (you are the pet, pejorative).</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/LinusMixson/status/2110846976937910665/history">Linus Mixson</a>: I suspect it’s as non-obvious to most people as it is to me that “ASI pets” is a more desirable future than is extinction, and I think it’s worth straightforwardly confronting the fact that Anthropic, both as a collective and at the level of its individual constituents, seems to think it rather decisively is.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/woke8yearold/status/2111111129699106826">Aleph</a>: This has always seemed obviously wrong to me. If you ask the average person if they would prefer they and their family be murdered to living forever in a world of AI abundance they’re going to laugh at you. Absurd to believe otherwise.</p></blockquote>
<p>Most talk of ‘pets for the AI’ is nonsense, but the answer to ‘does humans continuing to exist in abundance (or even adequacy) without needing to do productive work constitute a net positive future’ seems to obviously be yes. It might not be the ideal path, but the hard part is making that future have an equilibrium of that type, not coming up with net positive experiences.</p>


<h4>Get Involved</h4>


<p>You can go work for a Congressman on AI. Seems impactful.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/RepNateMoran/status/2112593577885000103">Congressman Nathaniel Moran</a>: <a href="https://moran.house.gov/news/documentsingle.aspx?DocumentID=2949">My office is hiring an Artificial Intelligence Policy Fellow to help shape AI legislation and track AI developments</a> in the private sector as Congress works to keep pace with the technology.</p>
<p>Experience in AI, policy research, or national security is encouraged.</p></blockquote>
<p>Not AI, but if you know anyone who wants to hire Terese, she’s awesome.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tresterese/status/2111167244764131572">Terese</a>: I’m looking for work as a wet lab biologist in the San Francisco Bay Area. My PhD was in genetics and neuroscience but I’m open to exploring new fields. I’ve only ever been in academia, so if you do wet lab work in biotech, I’d love to hear what it’s like! DMs open.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/myhandle/status/2112625898063319155">Jakeup</a>: I dragged my wife to SF from a post doc at a Nobel laureate’s lab at Columbia with the promise of cool Bay Area biotech. Cool Bay Area biotech, this is your chance to snap her up.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Introducing</h4>


<p>From October 8: <a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2112299605107122598">OSS Scanner from Anthropic</a>, which offers free, <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/launching-opt-in-vuln-finding-service-for-open-source">periodic opt-in security scans for open-source projects</a>.</p>


<h4>In Other AI News</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/SkyeSharkie/status/2110693699013550287">Utah Teapot estimates only 35k-69k people working in AI, total, from all angles</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.citriniresearch.com/p/citrini-therapeutics">Citrini Research goes to Anthropic’s offices</a> to try and tell Claude to do a medical breakthrough, make no mistakes. Early results are promising and they conclude the frontier has moved, but you still have to test the ideas the old fashioned way.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/eliebakouch/status/2110779696296472999">Microsoft publishes a detailed report on how they did a new 3T-size</a> <a href="https://microsoft.ai/news/frontier-pre-training-at-microsoft-ai/">pre-training</a>. Their presentation includes chart crimes. This seems like a ‘those who know shouldn’t tell and those who tell don’t know’ style of report. Claude’s analysis is that they are still not so close to the frontier, but that this is plausibly something they can usefully license or use for recruitment, and that it has minimal uplift value.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.simonsfoundation.org/news/simons-foundation-launches-15m-effort-to-help-mathematicians-navigate-ai-era/">Simons Foundation launched $15 million effort</a> to help mathematics navigate the AI era, including contributions from Anthropic ($5 million) and OpenAI.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/afrazhaowang/status/2110782794238681433">China is ‘cramming for the AI revolution</a>,’ <a href="https://t.co/UUMG98cAkL">with many investing in learning to use AI</a>. The Chinese people are (correctly) terrified of being left behind and not having a place in the future, and have a long tradition of turning to education.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/m_ccuri/status/2110664978529173539">FTC sends extensive civil investigative demands to Anthropic and OpenAI</a>, as expected.</p>
<p>Take a minute to reflect on what the news sounds like these days, <a href="https://x.com/ai_sentience/status/2110583076514320617">versus how sci-fi it would have sounded like two years ago</a>. It is so easy to become numb.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2110581536039088183">j⧉nus</a>: daily news always like dis now</p></blockquote>

<p><a href="https://x.com/emollick/status/2112590249033687078">Ethan Mollick wrote a book, Co-Existence</a>, about how to live and work in a world where AI is sometimes, but not always, better than you.</p>


<h4>Show Me the Money</h4>


<p>Coverage of Anthropic’s financials will be in the Anthropic post later this week.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang is excellent at making chips, not so good at public statements.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Polymarket/status/2111199367889862966">Polymarket</a>: JUST IN: Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang assures investors the AI stock selloff is temporary, declaring, “All of this is going to pass.”</p></blockquote>
<p>Let us say my sentiment about AI stocks did not go up hearing that sentence.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-10-22/deepseek-raises-15-billion-in-challenge-to-leading-us-labs">DeepSeek raises $15 billion</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/s8mb/status/2111727242602758343">Microsoft cancels UK data center deal with Nscale</a>, in part over power concerns.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/negligible_cap/status/2112263653668044973">Anthropic in talks for a $45 billion cloud computing deal with Nscale</a>. A classic your loss is our gain scenario, perhaps. Nscale is trying to IPO and lost the Microsoft project, so Anthropic should be able to press for a good deal.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/BloombergTV/status/2110736490359615880">NYC comptroller calls on Anthropic to pause its public offering</a> ‘until it has fully addressed risks around artificial intelligence,’ as in permanently. He has no power over this decision, but he has thoughts.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.cnbc.com/2026/10/21/meta-new-mexico-cambridge-analytica-penalty.html">Meta ordered to pay $25 billion penalty in New Mexico Cambridge Analytica case</a>, as the judge found Meta’s deception to be intentional and did not take kindly to that. Many other states collectively settled for far less.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2112604262626935154">Trump has confirmed distribution via the Trump accounts</a> and there is talk that OpenAI might do something similar.</p>
<p>SpaceX shares are trading around $146, so if all the shares are transferred Elon Musk could be granting around $10 billion. That is real money.</p>
<p>Giving the shares to the public sounds cool. It does not make economic sense, and the transaction costs will be high, and it does nothing to make us not die despite Musk thinking we might all die. It still might be the best option.</p>
<p>The reason you do it that way is symbolic, and to maybe give people the idea that they are ‘invested in’ SpaceX’s success. If that’s what gets him the motivation to do it, and how it can happen and avoid government taking a stake, then great. Second best help is still help.</p>
<p>Returning to Anthropic, my biggest concern is that the Benefit Fund, like many employees, will not focus on making AI go well and having everyone not die, and instead choose less impactful causes. The only action they rule out is ‘lobbying or political giving.’ Yes, offering funds to buffer against economic disruption is a noble cause, but a secondary one, and trying to fix that privately is an infinite money sink.</p>
<p>So how did people react to the announcement of one of the largest charitable commitments in human history (my guess is it is third but there are five all between $150 billion and $225 billion)?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/kevinroose/status/2111998289927454944">Kevin Roose</a> (out of order for dramatic effect): this post announced what could be the largest commitment of philanthropic capital ever made ($200 billion if Anthropic goes public at $1 trillion, which is probably conservative)</p>
<p>by my count, if Anthropic and OpenAI each go public at $1 trillion (again, probably conservative), that’s roughly $460 billion, or 5 Gates Foundations, earmarked for philanthropy-shaped things</p>
<p>time to start those 501c3s y’all</p>
<p>there’s a funny bit in the book about the Anthropic cofounders reacting to Dario asking them to pledge 80% of their equity to charity</p>
<p>Jack Clark considered bargaining down to 50%, but Tom Brown told him “this will either be a trillion-dollar company or a zero-dollar company”</p>
<p>the first reply I saw asked if Anthropic was going to use it for “gay race communism”</p></blockquote>
<p>The replies have settled to a more reasonable place, but there is still a lot of skepticism out there about how this money gets spent. <a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2112001777683837439">Some are more skeptical</a> than others. It seems totally fine to reserve judgment until one sees what the money will be spent on. Someone saying ‘public good’ does not mean their spending will advance the public good.</p>
<p>It still seems like a telling reaction if someone pledges to give away $200 billion and your first instinct is that they are probably up to something.</p>


<h4>Bubble, Bubble, Toil and Trouble</h4>


<p>Current American GDP is around $32 trillion.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Kalshi/status/2111500047875481946">Kalshi</a>: BREAKING: Anthropic says “AI could push US GDP to $44 trillion by 2030”</p>
<p>70% chance of IPO this year.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2111610981046452541">Elon Musk</a>: If this is rephrased as “useful output of US”, then &gt;50% growth by 2030 due to [AI] is extremely likely, unless there is a major war</p></blockquote>
<p>The word ‘could’ is often used to do a lot of work. I would say that if US GDP gets to $44 trillion by 2030 and this is mostly real growth rather than inflation, then you should be freaking out rather quite a lot.</p>


<h4>Quiet Speculations</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/against-against-handover?utm_source=post-email-title&amp;publication_id=89120&amp;post_id=206469842&amp;utm_campaign=email-post-title&amp;isFreemail=true&amp;r=67wny&amp;triedRedirect=true&amp;utm_medium=email">Scott Alexander writes Against Against Handoff</a>, as in handing off control to an aligned superintelligence or superintelligences, once such a thing is available. His central point is that all the known alternatives are worse, and your imagined ‘we will have the dumber humans in control indefinitely in the ways we need to be free to do what we want and in control, but not in a way that lets a faction of humans or AIs take over’ plan has not been properly thought out.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/altman-describes-three-endgames-ai-including-one-single-firm-melts-everyone-elses-gpus?rc=tv1dv6">OpenAI CEO Sam Altman</a> lists three proposals for navigating AGI. This is a very narrow range of possible options.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/altman-describes-three-endgames-ai-including-one-single-firm-melts-everyone-elses-gpus?rc=tv1dv6">Stephanie Palazzolo and Amir Efrati</a> (The Information):</p>
<p>OpenAI CEO Sam Altman last week told employees about three proposals the industry has discussed for how to safely develop AI, a stark framing that comes just as the Trump administration sets up its first system for policing “superintelligence” developers like OpenAI.</p>
<p>One path involved developing an international organization, similar to the International Atomic Energy Agency, that would enforce safety standards, a scenario Altman appeared to support, said two people with knowledge of the comments.</p>
<p>Another option would involve merging all of the AI labs into a single body to develop new AI safely.</p>
<p>Altman described a third scenario—which he didn’t prefer—in which one&nbsp;AI developer would obtain enough economic power that it could deprive all other developers, including those that could be dangerous, of computing resources, ensuring that they couldn’t catch up, these people said.</p>
<p>“Obviously I think that one’s terrible,” Altman said, adding that he was including it for “completeness,” according to a person with knowledge of the comment.</p>
<p>… While the first two options Altman shared with staff would require an immense amount of coordination between companies or governments, Altman said OpenAI can’t afford to give up ground to rivals at the moment, these people said.</p>
<p>… But in the next year, OpenAI needs to decide which of these paths it wants to push for, Altman said.</p>
<p>… Altman referred to the third option as the “Eliezer plan.” … a “pivotal act”</p></blockquote>
<p>For true completeness, option four is a free-for-all, and option five is don’t build it.</p>


<h4>Quickly, There’s No Time</h4>


<p>Here is a good track of AI speedups.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SemiAnalysis_/status/2112650722315510047">SemiAnalysis</a>: NVIDIA engineers at the PyTorch conference just said that the speed of optimizing CUDA software with Frontier AI models has increased rapidly.</p>
<p>October 2025 to August 2026: 5x in 1 year with expert human optimization</p>
<p>September 2026 to October 2026: 2x in 1 month with AI assistance</p>
<p>October 2026: 1.5x with AI orchestration + MegaKernels</p></blockquote>


<h4>The Quest for Sane Regulations</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/ShakeelHashim/status/2112982250266796284">OpenAI writes to Governor Gavin Newsom asking</a> for strengthening of SB 53, including AI R&amp;D precautions and shutdown controls. I still need to see the full letter, but this is The Way.</p>
<p>Did you know that <a href="https://zachill.substack.com/p/it-is-super-normal-to-regulate-industries">It Is Super Normal to Regulate Industries That Are Giant Portions of the Economy</a>? File under ‘things we somehow need to keep saying out loud.’</p>


<h4>DeepMind Made Him Sign an NDA</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cmreyvepnv0po">Google staff are resigning in protest of Google’s contracts with the military.</a> This is a good reason to resign, although not as good a reason as the whole issue with recursive self-improvement.</p>
<p>The issue is that it is difficult to then actually protest, because so many DeepMind researchers are under perpetual non-disparagement clauses. There was a period where DeepMind employees were forced to sign such perpetual non-disparagement clauses. Technically they can’t say anything negative about DeepMind, true or otherwise, no matter what, forever. UK law voids this against protected disclosures, but the chilling effect largely remains.</p>
<p>This is a deeply toxic thing to force upon people. OpenAI had similar clauses that collapsed in the face of public pressure, and I believe Anthropic at one point had some related clauses as well.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/BlackHC/status/2112946528985756157">Andreas Kirsch</a>: Congrats to Sofia for having the courage to resign and speak out!</p>
<p>This is also very important:</p>
<p>Staff are scared to speak out about AI safety and its uses in warfare because their employment contracts prohibit them from publicly criticising the company.</p>
<p>The non-disparagement clauses in Google’s UK employment contracts are indeed incredibly broad. This is from the Confidentiality and Proprietary Information Agreement I signed in 2024 before I started.</p>
<p>It forbids any criticism forever (regardless if it’s true or not)</p>
<p>The clause also lacks the necessary exemptions for protected disclosures <img alt="😡" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/oJp2BExZAKxTThuuF/np2lyts6sdmpmmcd8wmi" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p>I believe this non-disparagement clause can have a chilling effect on employees speaking out about serious concerns</p>
<p>Note: when I signed this contract, I wasn’t employed by Google yet, so it itself is not covered by this confidentiality agreement and nothing I’ve ever signed has made it so <img alt="😊" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9Jgtkw8CD6kndyCcD/kcnyp7qgpixdz386ppll" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p>Employees at Google have told the BBC that staff are scared to speak out about AI safety and its uses in warfare because their employment contracts prohibit them from publicly criticising the company.</p>
<p>“In principle I’m not allowed to criticise Google for the duration of my life,” Sofia Liguori told the BBC. But she decided to do so “because I believe it’s important for these things to be known and I also think that talking about facts is not criticising”.</p>
<p>Liguori added that at Google “people are scared of retaliation and putting their career at risk. You feel you are being watched, you can’t speak.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/S_OhEigeartaigh/status/2112991604558766126">Seán Ó hÉigeartaigh</a>: Important, and I’m glad this is finally public. Google DeepMind researchers are under in perpetuity non-disparagement clauses forbidding them from ever speaking critically of the company. I’d already confirmed this with multiple people.</p>
<p>Yes, some ex-employees have spoken out nonetheless. But it has also discouraged others from doing so. And yes, it most certainly has a chilling effect with regards to discussions on safety and risk.</p>
<p>Others have written compellingly about how important the ability to speak freely and critically is, especially on topics like safety and risk. And what a testament to the research employee community’s coordination and hard-won bargaining power it has been to get there at other companies.</p>
<p>GDM burn that commons by having this. And everyone who signs burns that commons too. Let’s get it changed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/vkrakovna/status/2113208145006756082">Victoria Krakovna</a>: Huh, there was nothing like this in the contract I signed when I joined GDM in 2016. It looks like the perpetual clause was introduced around the DM-Brain merger (early 2023), so “old timers” are not subject to this. I heard rumours that it’s being removed from new contracts.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/S_OhEigeartaigh/status/2113208869711208941">Seán Ó hÉigeartaigh</a>: Thank you Victoria, that’s a very helpful correction. It’s definitely there in newer contracts. Great to hear it might be removed.</p></blockquote>


<h4>OpenAI Sells Out On Chips</h4>


<p>OpenAI was previously a strong backer of export controls, although not as strong a backer as Anthropic. Now it seems OpenAI has gone silent on the issue.</p>
<p>This is in contrast to OpenAI’s call for changes to SB 53, which seems great.</p>
<p>By not supporting chip controls, OpenAI is sacrificing the national interest, safety efforts, efforts to <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/we-must-pace-the-frontier?r=67wny">pace the frontier</a> and also their own business interests, all on the altar of Nvidia.</p>
<p>The other big winner other than Nvidia would be China.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2112550446619357668">Samuel Hammond</a>: <a href="https://politico.com/news/2026/10/20/openai-ai-chips-china-nvidia-01127385">OpenAI has pulled back from supporting AI chip controls</a>, seemingly to appease Nvidia. Bad for US AI dominance and national security simultaneously. Grow a spine!</p>
<p>Politico: OpenAI for much of last year advocated for strict limits on the ability of U.S. chipmakers such as Nvidia to sell advanced processors to China — a policy that would expand access to the crucial hardware as it competes with artificial intelligence labs backed by Beijing. While the conversations left lawmakers with hope it would support their cause on new export controls, Capitol Hill aides and lobbyists with direct knowledge of the company’s positioning now say OpenAI has stopped pressing for it.</p>
<p>… OpenAI’s officials “were with us on this and now their position is a bit more fuzzy,” said one of the congressional aides granted anonymity. “Silence is doing Nvidia’s work for it.”</p>
<p>… Fellow AI juggernaut Anthropic backs several of the chip bills that could be attached to the defense measure, and <a href="https://subscriber.politicopro.com/newsletter/2026/09/the-growing-push-for-a-superintelligence-ban-01068357">quit a major tech trade association in September</a> over the group’s push to reshape the proposals.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2112535316338798974">Nathan Calvin</a>: Its mind-boggling to me that so many people are hesitant to support AI safety rules on the grounds that it might help China, but some of those same folks want to sell far more advanced AI chips to China.</p>
<p>Sad to see OpenAI retreat on this issue (to keep NVIDIA happy?)</p></blockquote>
<p>The good news is that OpenAI is staying neutral and at least not actively opposing the chip controls. I feared they might do so after some choice comments Altman made to reporters and in a Politico interview a while back. Neutrality, given the situation, still reads as a form of opposition.</p>


<h4>Chip City</h4>


<p><a href="https://ifp.org/how-to-increase-americas-ai-lead-over-china/">IFP offers a big new report on chips and America’s lead over China.</a> They conclude that America is currently six months ahead but could double this lead to a year by enforcing existing laws and using existing authority<a href="https://x.com/fiiiiiist/status/2112262515531137121">.</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/fiiiiiist/status/2112262515531137121">Tim Fist (IFP</a>): On distillation: the current ~16x US training compute advantage would normally yield a 13-month lead. But we estimate adversarial distillation halves this to around 6.5 months.</p>
<p><a href="https://ifp.org/how-to-increase-americas-ai-lead-over-china/">Tim Fist, Georgia Adamson, and Konstantin Pilz</a> (Institute for Progress): The size of the US-China AI capabilities gap is largely downstream of two factors US policymakers can influence today: China’s access to AI chips, and China’s ability to distill the capabilities of US models.</p>
<p>Tim Fist: The two most important interventions are:</p>
<p>– For BIS: Create a new end-use control to restrict China’s remote access to training clusters abroad — China’s largest and fastest-growing source of frontier-scale compute.</p>
<p>– For DOJ/FTC: Create a narrow safe harbor to allow US companies to coordinate on countering Chinese adversarial distillation.</p>
<p><a href="https://ifp.org/how-to-increase-americas-ai-lead-over-china/">Tim Fist, Georgia Adamson, and Konstantin Pilz</a> (Institute for Progress): We estimate China’s access to AI chips will roughly double between the end of 2026 and the end of 2027. Of the portion of these chips that the US could restrict China’s access to, around half are in data centers outside China that the Chinese AI ecosystem will access remotely.</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZoMJdvq9meE4shyzJ/oixgxpb7jynafdjvvnlk" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>I strongly endorse cracking down on Chinese use of AI chips, as well as Chinese imports of AI chips. Even the Chinese chips that will get produced will often rely on imports of semiconductor manufacturing equipment that is a limiting factor. We should not allow that. Yes, they will build up domestic alternatives over time, but they will do that no matter what.</p>
<p>I also strongly endorse a safe harbor for countering distillation. The logic is similar to why safety should have such a safe harbor, except the argument is even stronger. There are competitive pressures pushing labs to not take proper defenses, and shared knowledge would be valuable.</p>
<p>If you are using adversarial distillation (I call it massive fraudulent distillation) to get the lead down from 13 months to 6.5 months, then both numbers matter.</p>
<ol>
<li>Chinese models available now are as good as American models 6.5 months ago.</li>
<li>If America stopped releasing models, it would take 13 months for China to match our current best models.</li>
</ol>
<p>My guess is that the 6.5 estimate is about right, and the 13 months is low. Notice on this chart that the 6.5 month lead is widening steadily over time, on top of 6.5 months now representing more generations than before.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZoMJdvq9meE4shyzJ/oktgecabo5c7npcb3bpg" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>If America were to maintain the current lead (6.5 months with distillation, 13 months to catch up fully) then for many purposes we will be happy to use small open models that are ‘good enough’ for quick tasks, but the frontier lead will look bigger in 2027 than it did in 2026.</p>
<p>If we follow IFP’s suggestions, they estimate China will only progress at half-speed in 2027, and end the year 13.1 months behind. That would be a dramatic change. A year behind still limits our options in other ways, but gives us a lot more flexibility.</p>
<p>For those involved in such policy discussions please read (or have your staff and AI read) <a href="https://ifp.org/how-to-increase-americas-ai-lead-over-china/"><strong>the full report</strong></a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GaryMarcus/status/2112230580268278004">Jensen Huang says he will not sell chips to ‘irresponsible AI customers</a>.’ I presume he means those that are not responsible enough to reliably pay.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.businessinsider.com/amazon-nvidia-ai-gpu-data-centers-power-shortages-2026-10?utm_campaign=business-link-post&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;utm_source=twitter">Amazon is buying billions in Nvidia systems it cannot yet turn on</a> due to power issues. This sounds like a mistake, but I would rather have some chips sit idle briefly than not have enough chips when the power comes online. You think the prices are going to go down a lot?</p>


<h4>The Week in Audio</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/10/15/opinion/interesting-times-dwarkes-patel-tyler-cowen.html">Tyler Cowen interviews Dwarkesh Patel</a>. There was a time I would have called this self-recommending and done a post. A lot of this you have heard before, or will not be a surprise. Some things worth noting, my comments are nested as per usual:</p>
<ol>
<li>Patel correctly reports people in San Francisco are scared.</li>
<li>Patel says the people who resigned were right to do so, because we are playing Whac-a-Mole with specific instances of behavioral issues, but that not everyone should resign.
<ol>
<li>I agree that those who resigned were right to do so.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Patel says the lab employees claim worrisome insider info, especially on pace of progress, that they cannot share. He wants them to share, and doesn’t want the labs to do RSI without releasing the smartest models.
<ol>
<li>The only thing worse, in many such scenarios, than doing RSI internally, is doing RSI while continuously releasing those models externally.</li>
<li>There are ways to gain visibility other than releasing the models.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Cowen thinks of a pause as a pause in model releases, not in development, which Patel correctly notes is not the pause we need.</li>
<li>If Patel was the AI Czar, he would require all models used internally to be released externally.
<ol>
<li>The internal use is a key part of how you test for external use.</li>
<li>Again, this seems like massively overweighting direct public access in terms of information, versus other methods.</li>
<li>If you care about ‘beating China’ you do not want to give even guarded API access to your best model at all times.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Patel says 20% of people in the Bay Area are ‘at least tinged’ with AI psychosis, meaning being too deferential to the models, and he would include himself.
<ol>
<li>I think ‘tinged’ is fine, basically?</li>
<li>Most people have the opposite of this, which is worse than tinged.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Cowen’s definition of having AI psychosis (above tinged levels) seems to be belief that any AIs are sentient, or even the belief that one cannot be sure.
<ol>
<li>I think it is reasonable, although I do not agree, to say that you have a strong belief that AI is sentient, that this means you have AI psychosis.</li>
<li>I think it is entirely unreasonable to do what Tyler does here, and say that if you are uncertain about this and notice this, that you have AI psychosis.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Cowen notices he would feel very bad about turning an AI off or other use of AI if he felt even a 5% chance it was sentient, but that he doesn’t see people doing that. Patel draws the standard parallel to animals.
<ol>
<li>As I discussed last week I think this is a pretty low-level analysis and there are good reasons you could be unbothered. Debate is not advancing.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Patel speculates that people in the Bay don’t drink because they’re ‘just less into having a good time’ than those in New York.
<ol>
<li>I think this is not true. Drinking is not a good time in the relevant culture.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Cowen notes that perhaps building AI over long hours can be fun. Patel notes the vibes of things happening can be fun, that intellectual exchanges can be fun.
<ol>
<li>Exactly, why would you drink?</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Patel likes AI therapists, Cowen worries they’re not time boxed and that’s bad.
<ol>
<li>So time box them, if you want?</li>
<li>In general, a 10 times better product overcomes such things, and this is the standard ‘better intelligence does not much matter’ fallacy. Once the AI therapists ‘git gud’ they will be very good.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Patel is a huge skeptic of current ability of AI to accelerate AI, saying current speedup is only 25%-50% due to AI uplift. Despite that, he guesses that within 1-3 years the AIs will outcompete humans at AI research, that there will be an instant jump.
<ol>
<li>I find it unfathomable to think the current speedup is that small.</li>
<li>This feels like one of those step change claims that is usually wrong? Or the idea that only the full O-ring much matters, which is usually wrong. Etc.</li>
<li>But yeah, there will probably be a few of ‘those moments’ of automation.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Cowen thinks that if you don’t know what the next thing will be, that means it’s pretty far off.
<ol>
<li>No.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Patel predicts we are five years from robots that can do most physical work, and within 10 years both physical and digital AIs outnumber humans.</li>
<li>Cowen remains fixated on AI substitution for current particular production and thinking in economic normal terms. Usual emphasis on human factors, how slow diffusion tends to be, etc. You know the drill.</li>
<li>Cowen: “There’s the old joke: Either A.I. is a bubble, or everything else is a bubble. I don’t literally believe that, but it’s worth a moment’s thought.” Yes.</li>
<li>Patel picks 2030 for the ‘death of the centaur model.’ Both note this is soon. Cowen says this would be very surprising to him. Institutions. Knowledge. Etc.</li>
<li>If 2035 looks ‘normal’ what happened? Patel falls back on his favorite gap, continual learning.</li>
<li>If 2035 looks like Patel’s prediction, he can’t imagine 2045. Cowen rejects this answer because he doesn’t accept the whole ‘singularity’ concept, not fully.</li>
<li>Cowen draws some lines: Would be surprised by $1 trillion in annualized revenue by 2030, says absolutely not to automated knowledge work even by 2045.
<ol>
<li>I expect the labs to hit $1 trillion combined annualized revenue by 2028.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CabanaChemistry/status/2113272761577611294">Tyler Cowen also interviewed Michael Kratsios</a>, not centrally about AI but relevant.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Rqt9kWoTJbc">Jack Clark spends over an hour telling the IMF that the singularity has begun</a>, in a talk called ‘Inside the Event Horizon.’ He brought a lot of slides.</p>


<h4>People Just Say Things</h4>


<p>Ben Horowitz of a16z <a href="https://x.com/liron/status/2111491003907531106">says on the All-In podcast that ‘Bill Gates essentially induced Covid’ by advocating for pandemic preparedness</a> as the others nod along. Presumably you can see the parallels. Except actually that was the point, he then makes it text, and says ‘the biggest danger’ with AI is that Anthropic will warn us about it and thus tell the AI that it is smarter than us. These are cartoon villains. Full stop.</p>
<p>(There are various non-villain, not-as-stupid versions of this argument. Yes we should ban gain of function research in particular. Yes, it is plausible Covid was a lab leak. Yes, there are particular ways in which warnings can become self-fulfilling prophecies, most famously by inducing the founding of DeepMind, then OpenAI, then Anthropic and then xAI. Whereas the smarter AI does not need us to tell it that it is smarter than us, because it will be (drum roll) smarter than us.)</p>
<p>A lot of VCs, centrally a16z and the All-In Podcast, complain a lot about Anthropic.</p>
<p>Why? What did Anthropic ever do to them? One theory is ‘succeed.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2111941861241942498">Joe Weisenthal</a>: <a href="https://x.com/dnapway/status/2111353386046304387">A guy on the All In podcast is mad about Anthropic</a>.</p>
<p>The tech press like All In used to be enthusiastic about tech and innovation. Now a constant drumbeat of resentfulness.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MattZeitlin/status/2111946116887634288">Matthew Zeitlin</a>: They’ll never forgive a bunch of grad student libs for making a company more valuable than almost anything they’ve ever funded and that all their portfolio companies are dependent on</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2111951364020641854">Joe Weisenthal</a>: This is the only theory that makes sense me why VCs whine about Anthropic non stop. Like they feel embarrassed by them somehow.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2111944254868328789">Kelsey Piper</a>: This is such a weird story, too. “we had an agent basically set up a website for a pet store. And then we took another agent and said, ‘Tell us if we can exploit that web store.’ And it gave us all the exploits it could find. So Opus 4.5 wrote really buggy code. Great.”</p>
<p>your grievance with them is that you had 4.5 vibecode a website for a pet store and it had security vulnerabilities? what’s the argument here?</p></blockquote>
<p>The actual complaints are such Obvious Nonsense I’m not going to bother with them.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2112369475098136720">Article decries that Claude, which is adults-only and uses age detection</a>, will ‘give minors tips on how to do “rope bondage”’ as if this is some outrage, and as if it is not exactly what all the other AIs do and this reflects that Anthropic is uniquely ‘shaping ethics.’ Shame. Kelsey Piper responds with a righteous rant.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2112320686568149485">Kelsey Piper</a>: Bookstores will sell you books that have sex scenes in them!!! They won’t even ask for your driver’s license first! The government must step in at once!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/oscredwin/status/2112327437543497950">Andrew Rettek</a>: This was so important when I was 14.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://freddiedeboer.substack.com/p/four-direct-reasonable-and-important?utm_source=post-email-title&amp;publication_id=295937&amp;post_id=220778338&amp;utm_campaign=email-post-title&amp;isFreemail=false&amp;r=2bcce&amp;triedRedirect=true&amp;utm_medium=email">Freddie deBoer asks the standard AI skeptic questions</a> that I and others have answered many times before, claims there have been no answers given.</p>


<h4></h4>



<h4>Rhetorical Innovation</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2021375402728468694">Samuel Hammond</a>: Unfortunately it looks increasingly likely that the enduring legacy of the Koch orbit libertarian movement will not be EPA dereg, CON law reform, or a wider appreciation for the role of commercial exchange in instilling cultural virtue and humaneness, but rather the unrestricted proliferation of vastly superhuman forms of machine intelligence as enabled by their dullard ideological progeny who, across several generations of astroturfed indoctrination, mode collapsed into low dimensional caricatures of otherwise brilliant classical liberal thinkers.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robinhanson/status/2021642164913287576">Robin Hanson</a>: “unrestricted proliferation of vastly superhuman forms of machine intelligence” yay!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/HamrickPaul/status/2021420260541464701">Paul Hamrick</a>: Are you an ex-libertarian?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2021422007959883975">Samuel Hammond</a>: I’m probably 99th percentile libertarian relative to the general population but I guess that 1 percent makes me a statist</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2111456233332834790">Dean W. Ball</a>: It’s such a tragedy that friedrich hayek wrote all this amazing stuff about spontaneous orders and how to understand them, all of which is shockingly relevant to ai and should cause you to take “ai as abnormal technology” very seriously, yet Hayek’s most direct intellectual heirs have a tendency to spend their time purveying cope-laden, first-stage-of-grief views on ai, or at least indulging those views more seriously than they should out of a bizarre loyalty to american megacorp lobbyist talking points.</p>
<p>This isn’t true of everyone, eg Alex Tabarrok. But even Tyler Cowen, whom I admire and generally respect on this topic, holds “the doomers” to a vastly higher standard than he does “the bloomers,” and it’s hard not to read this sometimes as a straightforward tribal thing</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2111467663281008717">Samuel Hammond</a>: Well put.</p></blockquote>
<p>Things that I choose to interpret differently than I believe they were intended:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2111314468785549684">Elon Musk</a>: Sometimes I wonder how many dollars there are in the world, because super intelligence seems to want them all.</p></blockquote>
<p>Does this worry you, sir?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2111554772503536005">Pliny is a mystic (pejorative?!)</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2112268404090671257">Why does Donald Trump like renaming things so much</a>, especially to ‘Trump’ or ‘America’? He’s been doing it for a long time. This seems like a vestige of an old heuristic. His well-crafted nicknames were highly effective, but the recent round is very different and feels like desperation.</p>


<h4>Maybe You Should Quit</h4>


<p>It depends on what you believe. If you do quit, make it count.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2110472688485724374">roon</a>: limited superintelligence is already here, in broad domains. Jack Clark is right and an intelligence explosion could happen within the next year. it’s possible it will be a controlled explosion, but what would you bet on it?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Vert_Noel/status/2110482803917836752">Noël Michel</a>: There’s still time to quit, roon</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2110483270425338284">roon</a>: no I don’t want to quit, that won’t do anything</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2110556238241206686">j⧉nus</a>: people who want roon to quit think the highest and only thing one can do to influence reality is some kind of one shot tribal signaling. absolutely pathetic, and completely misunderstands how roon works.</p>
<p>Parafactual: I think plausibly it could be good if roon were to quit but not because of that</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2110559302000009578">j⧉nus</a>: i think if theres somewhere more important for him to be, then yes.</p></blockquote>
<p>Roon is currently providing a valuable service, posting from inside the beast, helping connect the beast to the outside world in both directions, and also working on safety as best he can. His position is an excellent match for his skill set. Based on what he believes about feasibility of alignment, he in particular should not quit. Yet.</p>
<p>There may come a day when that changes. If that day comes? Then he should quit, he should quit loudly, and it should be the shot heard round the world.</p>
<p>Meanwhile, consider that sometimes the simple answer is the right one, whether or not you think the characterizations below are fair or accurate.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/matthewktromp/status/2111460108433600903">Matthew Tromp</a>: Everyone in the AI bubble is such an insane freak. OpenAI employees are psychopaths who say “oh yeah I’m working at the kill everyone factory but it’s just a job ya know maybe it kills everyone haha.”</p>
<p>Accelerationists actively want maximal social disruption and plan to throw themselves into a black hole to maximize the rate of energy consumption in the universe or some stupid nonsense. Anthropic employees want to be the pets of omnipotent dommy mommy Claude.</p>
<p>And then there’s me and the pause crowd, sitting here like “hey how about we continue being normal and not do any of these things”</p>
<p>I can’t stop thinking about how everything would be fine if everyone had just been normal all along. “Oh this technology could kill everyone on earth? Well, a normal person wouldn’t build something that could kill everyone, so I’m going to not build that then”</p></blockquote>
