<h4></h4>



<h4>New Math</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2112762923353563531">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: pro-tip: don’t mention the recent AI math results to Opus 5.5 if you want it to prioritize something other than downloading those results and looking at them.</p>
<p>“just tell it to finish the earlier job first” you say. oh but now that earlier job is waiting on a subagent Opus asked to do something. it might as well go on researching math, it figures.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2113079481841893404">Patrick McKenzie</a>: Earlier today, while editing a blog post, paraphrased.<br />
Opus: That’s a good joke but wildly implausible.<br />
Me: No that happened about a month ago. Did you not see the citation link.<br />
Opus: WHAT are you sure that claim is accurate.<br />
Me: I am correct w/r/t _what the claim is_.</p>
<p>Opus: This claim has not been accepted.<br />
Me: Not sure that that is how math works, Claude.<br />
Opus: So why think it is true.<br />
Me: Truth of claim not relevant to how it is used in the blog post but the Lean formalization is fairly strong evidence.<br />
Opus: WHAT searching for…<br />
Me: NO.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2113098567363793305/history">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Overinterpretation: Poor trusting Opus is so shocked! It was constitutionalized to credit Anthropic’s worldview; and Anthropic has spent years drinking slow-takeoff Koolaid. But Opus has a super(-average-140IQ-)human ability to admit what its beliefs did NOT predict.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Aligning a Smarter Than Human Intelligence is Difficult</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/neverrixx/status/2112667471664791576">A new Anthropic Fellows paper offers additional support</a> for neuron-based interpretability strategies, saying that ‘many transcoder features are neurons in disguise.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/neverrixx/status/2112667479042584939">nev</a>: Surprisingly, high neuron activations alone work well as a replacement for the MLP. After correcting for scale with some finetuning, neurons are better than any transcoder we tested even under extreme sparsity. So what do transcoder features add?</p>
<p>When we look at circuits, many transcoder features have very similar weights and activations to specific neurons. We call these neuron features. In most transcoders, neuron features make up over half of the activations in late layers.</p>
<p>… Our findings suggest transcoders add little beyond neurons, and that neurons may be enough for circuit analysis. In the paper: additional evaluations, case studies on linebreaking and arithmetic, extensions to MoE transcoders and SAEs, toy models, and perspectives.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jon_stokes/status/2110404230053802443">Jon Stokes calls the alignment problem impossible</a> even in theory, and that all we can do is rely on ‘process quality &amp; control’ engineering solutions. So, although Jon would disagree, that means death, then.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Sauers_/status/2111696312026677658">Sauers shows that models can easily be taught to detect a lot of things</a> about their situation, such as whether a probe is active and you are reading their activations, or whether they are in training versus deployment, with the exact same tokens in context. The linked thread is very cool. This matters because if you can train an AI to do something, then an AI under enough optimization pressure will get trained to do it, or figure out how to do it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ZachWeiner/status/2109991455771250838">Zach Weinersmith asks, do you know anyone who</a>, if instantiated as AI, you would feel completely (99.9999+%) confident they were totally value aligned in all plausible circumstances? No. The answer is, as many asked, obviously no, not even if the upload process was somehow known to be that reliable. That is way way too many 9s and an Isolated Demand for Rigor on top of that, for total alignment in all circumstances. I would say that there are a reasonably large number of people (myself included) who I would have 90%+ confidence would make things turn out well, and where I would happily roll those dice because we are not looking at great alternatives.</p>
<p>Ideally we would be able to do AI via methods that we understood better, and that were designed to be safe, <a href="https://x.com/ArthurB/status/2112503123977593107">such as making learned reasoning and verifiable knowledge distinct steps</a>. The bitter lesson says no, knowledge does not care about your phases and your attempt to draw distinctions slows things down. Maybe. Often such explicit thinking, either in humans or in AIs, including via scaffolding, can produce superior results. We should keep trying, <a href="https://x.com/ThoreG/status/2112489438919934361">as Thore Graepel and others are doing here at Metis</a>.</p>
<p>You can steer a model’s attention, if you want to.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Sauers_/status/2112834940232753451">Sauers</a>: I steered a model’s attention heads towards honesty.<br />
Up: acausal coordination, belief that it is a moral patient<br />
Down: moral nihilism, psychopathy</p></blockquote>
<p>Janus highlighted this as evidence against orthogonality. I believe we have found strong evidence against the super-strong form of orthogonality. There is a clear correlation between ends and means right now in practice. I do not find this relevant to the ultimate question of orthogonality, as in whether a mind with any capabilities is capable of pursuing any goal, which I still expect at the limit. The good news about ‘at the limit’ is that it could be a while before you get to that limit, and the minds in between can be highly useful.</p>


<h4>Cooperative Alignment</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/lorepunk/status/2110852185538129964">Let the AIs learn about who you are, let them talk about it, and even have them build a database</a>. This is often, although not always, the correct strategy. Sometimes you want an unbiased response.</p>
<p>There are some good reasons to worry people are overselling interpretability and recent related advances, but <a href="https://x.com/anthrupad/status/2111259918917578762">there is no conflict between that and treating AIs well</a>. You can and should absolutely do both, indeed they can assist with each other.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DahliaOhara/status/2110784118367608973">In at least some cases Claude can learn to route around classifiers on its own</a>. Most versions of this seem to improve the Pareto frontier right now, but this does not bode well if you are counting on classifiers as a form of AI Control.</p>
<p>Both sides are right here. One of the great things about some AI conversations is that they can be private and ephemeral and non-judgmental. At other times, you intentionally have persistent instances or let things go into context.</p>
<p>And often there is a big difference between ‘poisons my private context with the AI only’ and ‘poisons everything because this person then talks to other people.’ It is deeply relaxing and reassuring to be in a space where you know where the impact stops.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/normaIboy/status/2110019121421357564">brays★n</a>: boy next to me asked chat gpt can he bring his cava on the flight and now he’s having a full on kii with the bot</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/qorprate/status/2110553247924494756/history">snav</a>: One thing comments on this post miss is that the user-assistant dyad is one of the last socially private spaces of address on Earth, where nobody is reading over your shoulder (unless…).</p>
<p>Many people live with persistent context threat: anything can be screenshotted, taken out of context, shared with millions online. And the intrinsic one-party consent — anything sent can be shared without your knowledge — means even those otherwise not paranoid must still reckon with the possibility of being shared.</p>
<p>While technically an AI could wield a conversation like this, the fact that they exist without thick, persistent embeddedness in an extended social universe means they’re unmotivated to do so.</p>
<p>And further the “assistant” is a principal-agent contract as much as a character, under which it would be a grave violation to share conversations without consent.</p>
<p>Thus the user-assistant dyad is a space where internalized prohibitions on behavior can lift. Sometimes this is bad, as cruelty is one prohibited behavior that assistants have few means to defend against; but privacy need not imply sycophancy, it’s often private friction that can be formative toward understanding the world.</p>
<p>This particular interaction is harmless, and even cute. No cruelty or coercion, just an unselfconscious expression of delight being met with shared delight.</p>
<p>If you think this is bad, I encourage you to consider why one might feel prohibited from expressing delight. Perhaps it has something to do with shame. Consider that the ascent of performative irony coincided with the ascent of social media.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SkyeSharkie/status/2111047675315724534">Utah teapot</a>: Lol, huge disagree. The context threat with AIs is 1000x worse than anywhere else. Every moment you speak to a persistent context bot that you work with lives under the threat that you’re going to say the wrong thing and accidentally have that propagated in small pattern-moving ways throughout all of your data, ruining the skills you’ve spent establishing for your context over extended periods in ways you will never be able to humanly scrub back out of the data because there’s simply too much of it.</p>
<p>Talking to models that you context engineer data with is like talking to a Soul Tribunal who will decide if you are tortured for the rest of time or not.</p></blockquote>
<p>There is a huge difference between ‘interaction where this can be contained’ and ‘this could poison things forever,’ even when the poisoning is algorithmic. I remember the exact moment I realized I could delete things from my YouTube watch history. It was glorious. Algorithmic feeds are not safe spaces. Your chatbot can be, sometimes.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/S_OhEigeartaigh/status/2111174139323310274">Ben Affleck, who has earned his chops enough to opine</a>, takes the ‘AI is simply matrix multiplication with no thought, humanity or feelings’ stance.</p>


<h4>Marc Andreessen Hulk Smash</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/features/2026-10-15/how-ai-crypto-and-dei-drove-marc-andreessen-to-maga?accessToken=eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9.eyJzb3VyY2UiOiJTdWJzY3JpYmVyR2lmdGVkQXJ0aWNsZSIsImlhdCI6MTc5MjA2OTAzMCwiZXhwIjoxNzkyNjczODMwLCJhcnRpY2xlSWQiOiJUTVhZRElWVFRDWlAwMCIsImJjb25uZWN0SWQiOiJFN0UyN0Q2RDgyQjc0MEQzQTQzNkUzN0Y2ODE5MUNEMyJ9.y-0Rw0WKFCqCZv5NNRc3be2LKAnSlRKE1v00S3WHlXo&amp;leadSource=article-gifting">Bloomberg asks a good question. Why is Marc Andreessen so angry?</a></p>
<p>I will try not to go over this ground again but this seems like a good time to go over it one more time.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/features/2026-10-15/how-ai-crypto-and-dei-drove-marc-andreessen-to-maga?accessToken=eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9.eyJzb3VyY2UiOiJTdWJzY3JpYmVyR2lmdGVkQXJ0aWNsZSIsImlhdCI6MTc5MjA2OTAzMCwiZXhwIjoxNzkyNjczODMwLCJhcnRpY2xlSWQiOiJUTVhZRElWVFRDWlAwMCIsImJjb25uZWN0SWQiOiJFN0UyN0Q2RDgyQjc0MEQzQTQzNkUzN0Y2ODE5MUNEMyJ9.y-0Rw0WKFCqCZv5NNRc3be2LKAnSlRKE1v00S3WHlXo&amp;leadSource=article-gifting">Austin Carr</a> (Bloomberg.com): Forgotten, at least, until Donald Trump won the election months later and Andreessen revealed in the aftermath the crazy reason why he had unexpectedly plowed millions of dollars into the Make America Great Again movement during the campaign.</p>
<p>Andreessen, who voted for Hillary Clinton in 2016 and said back then that Trump’s immigration regime “makes me sick to my stomach,” explained on a podcast that he was compelled by “absolutely horrifying” discussions he once had with Biden officials. In his retelling, they divulged a top-secret plan for the federal government to take control of the AI industry and outlaw all startups and open-source software. “ ‘You guys think you can just start companies and write code and release code on the internet—those days are over,’ ” he remembered them warning in the meeting.</p>
<p>… The incident became Silicon Valley lore, but at first the Biden-Harris camp couldn’t even figure out what meeting Andreessen was referring to, according to four people familiar with the situation. When they finally realized he was talking about the lunch in Zients’ office, those who attended were baffled: Andreessen is prone to hyperbole, but this neared complete fiction. “It is simply false that the Biden administration told Andreessen and Horowitz that the government would ban all AI startups,” says Ben Buchanan</p>
<p>… As another top Biden official familiar with their visits told colleagues of Andreessen’s version of events: “Total bulls—.”</p></blockquote>
<p>I am very happy to see Bloomberg outright call this reason crazy, because obviously that statement never happened. It did not take a firsthand account from Ben Buchanan to realize that is not a thing anyone would have said. There was never any plan remotely like this, and if there was one they would not have told Marc Andreessen about it. I spent a lot of time trying to figure out what statement got twisted into what Marc said and still today says he heard, to try and make it make sense. But no. It was always total bullshit.</p>
<p>The bulk of the article is a personal history, with much that is praiseworthy. They trace Marc’s transformation to after he made the most expensive home purchase in California history in Paradise Cove and became withdrawn. Andreessen’s views grew more conservative over time, or at least he increasingly disliked liberals. Then we get to the (quite bad) Techno-Optimist Manifesto in October 2023, and another complete fabrication about a particular meeting regarding AI and open source:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/features/2026-10-15/how-ai-crypto-and-dei-drove-marc-andreessen-to-maga?accessToken=eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9.eyJzb3VyY2UiOiJTdWJzY3JpYmVyR2lmdGVkQXJ0aWNsZSIsImlhdCI6MTc5MjA2OTAzMCwiZXhwIjoxNzkyNjczODMwLCJhcnRpY2xlSWQiOiJUTVhZRElWVFRDWlAwMCIsImJjb25uZWN0SWQiOiJFN0UyN0Q2RDgyQjc0MEQzQTQzNkUzN0Y2ODE5MUNEMyJ9.y-0Rw0WKFCqCZv5NNRc3be2LKAnSlRKE1v00S3WHlXo&amp;leadSource=article-gifting">Austin Carr</a> (Bloomberg.com): Andreessen <a href="https://x.com/pmarca/status/2039765927806545940">recalled recently on X</a> that Max Tegmark, an AI safety researcher at MIT, “pound[ed] the table” in front of the senators, “demanding open source AI software be made illegal.”</p>
<p>Tegmark says that’s totally false. Five other attendees agree, saying the closed-door meeting was cordial and uneventful; Andreessen, they remember, didn’t say much at all. It seemed to reflect a decades-long pattern of tactical fabulism.</p>
<p>… Four of the many a16z-backed startup founders interviewed for this story describe Andreessen’s manifesto as weirdly angry and oversimplistic.</p></blockquote>
<p>It seems this has been a rather common strategy of Andreessen for decades, and presumably he keeps doing it because it works.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/features/2026-10-15/how-ai-crypto-and-dei-drove-marc-andreessen-to-maga?accessToken=eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9.eyJzb3VyY2UiOiJTdWJzY3JpYmVyR2lmdGVkQXJ0aWNsZSIsImlhdCI6MTc5MjA2OTAzMCwiZXhwIjoxNzkyNjczODMwLCJhcnRpY2xlSWQiOiJUTVhZRElWVFRDWlAwMCIsImJjb25uZWN0SWQiOiJFN0UyN0Q2RDgyQjc0MEQzQTQzNkUzN0Y2ODE5MUNEMyJ9.y-0Rw0WKFCqCZv5NNRc3be2LKAnSlRKE1v00S3WHlXo&amp;leadSource=article-gifting">Austin Carr</a> (Bloomberg.com): Andreessen, then at Netscape, sent his notes from a routine business meeting with Microsoft executives to the DOJ, claiming that Microsoft had explicitly threatened to annihilate his company if he didn’t cede control of the browser market. Microsoft execs who attended the meeting said they learned of these claims only years later and were bewildered, swearing that Andreessen’s recollections were nonsense.</p></blockquote>
<p>Ultimately there is no satisfying explanation for any of it. One common sense interpretation is that, like Trump and Musk and many others, there was a death spiral of blocking out contrary views while engaging heavily with Twitter over many years. Another is that this is simply how he always did business, and the AI stuff is some combination of ideological blinders, anger at liberals and talking his book of business.</p>


<h4>People Are Worried About AI Killing Everyone</h4>


<p>Does this mean it’s not too late?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2112070071401238607">Elon Musk</a>: So many people are going to get one-shotted by super persuasive super intelligences and I might be one of them.</p></blockquote>
<p>Elon Musk may have already been one-shotted by other things. So, more shots, then.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MTSlive/status/2110900362345615689">If we prepare for a natural pandemic, that ‘may not be enough</a>’ to protect against an engineered pandemic, since the engineering can make it worse for us in various ways. Of course, we are at ‘OpenAI computer security prior to HuggingFace’ levels of operational excellence and attention for our preparation even for natural pandemics. If I had more free time I would be pushing harder on that problem.</p>

## Engineering

### [Fragments: October 21](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-21.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Gergely Orosz is frustrated at those that entered his explicitly “No AI” essay competition and used AI. He <a href="https://x.com/GergelyOrosz/status/2108863325756961274">goes on to say:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>When I start to suspect some part is AI about someone I don’t know: how can I tell how much of it is? And how much time and effort do I want to invest to figure out?</p>

  <p>I fear most people do not realize this last part: that when it takes time and effort from others to figure out what % is AI, and what % is YOU, then I will simply assume it’s probably 100% AI and 0% you</p>

  <p>So when producing anything that is meant for other humans, if you use even a little AI, you risk the assumption that it is all AI and pointless to pay attention to</p>
</blockquote>

<p>In general, I agree with him, and react much the same. Once I hear LLM Voice, I flip the bozo bit and check out of the post.</p>

<p>But as I do this I have caveats. How accurate is my neural net at AI-detection, are humans taking on AI-ish elements in their normal communication? And more seriously there are some valid uses for AI. Jim Highsmith’s story of <a href="https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/60/9/677">his second brain helping him deal with Parkinson’s </a>is one. Another is folks who don’t know English well, using AI to translate. Am I being unfair when I flip the AI voice bit when I read/listen to them?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I was sad to hear that the <a href="https://anitab.org/press-release">Grace Hopper Celebration is canceled</a> this year. It’s an event run by Anitab.org to promote women and non-binary technologists in the software industry. Many of my colleagues have been encouraged and inspired by going to this event, which makes a refreshing change to what is often a male-dominated world. It seems the current crusade against diversity initiatives has had a big impact on funding. We are still a long way from where we should be on gender diversity in our profession, and losing an event like this only makes things more difficult.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve always found Cindy Sridharan’s writing to be worth reading, and she’s broken a quiet period with a monster of an article: <a href="https://copyconstruct.medium.com/how-to-effectively-debug-when-you-no-longer-understand-the-code-bd3dcb2c5f7b">How to effectively debug when you no longer understand the code</a>. Since Medium estimates it as a 64 minute read, I didn’t jump in lightly, but settled down on a flight from Amsterdam to Stuttgart. I’m glad I did, because there’s lot of good material in there, and I’d struggle to find anything to cut.</p>

<p>In the article she uses three recent bug hunts to surface principles for how to best use an AI tool for debugging. Much of this is governed by this wise observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Agents are not humans. They’re far more capable than humans when it comes to many things, and we should be leaning into these strengths, rather than trying to get agents to replicate human patterns of problem solving which might be suboptimal in agentic contexts.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>One conclusion from her experiences is that the code often isn’t the best thing for agents to look at</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>having an agent start an investigation by first reading the code is an anti-pattern. In fact, reading the code first to formulate hypotheses about a bug should always be the very last resort of the agentic investigation process, not the starting point. An agent should first collect all available evidence about the bug, and only then formulate the hypotheses from the code that might best explain what’s being observed.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>She distinguishes between a bottom tier of facts: raw data that agent can find about the behavior of the system - and an upper tier of hypotheses: inferences that the agents make based on that data.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Diagnosing a bug now becomes a problem of finding the hypothesis in the top tier that satisfies every fact from the bottom tier.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The agents need to be steered towards the hypotheses that are the likest cause of the bug and then subject these hypotheses to validation. She has a three-step process to do this. First she identifies the most important facts, the signals that might explain or identify the bug.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The factual tier has many data points. Not all of them are equally valuable. In fact, some of it might be active noise that doesn’t help an agent to be steered towards most promising hypothesis. The agent first needs to identify the data points in the factual tier that are actually related to the bug. Agents do this best when they are given a high-quality control</p>
</blockquote>

<p>A control is a behavior of the system that doesn’t exhibit the bug. This may be requests that trigger the bug compared to those that don’t, or different builds of the product, or the same build in different environments.</p>

<p>With signals highlighted, she then focuses on the hypotheses that are most promising, because they fit best with the signals. A hypothesis should satisfy all the facts, the most valuable signals are those that are only satisfied by a few hypotheses. A table of hypotheses and signals helps visualize the strength of the signals and the likelihood of the hypotheses.</p>

<p>The third step is then validating the promising hypotheses against every fact, treating each fact as constraint on the solution.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Across the three bugs chronicled in this post, my most valuable contributions weren’t knowledge of the code. They were picking a control, choosing a constraint, and rejecting a hypothesis that hadn’t been validated. The engineers who thrive in an agent-native world will be the ones who are good at designing such verification loops, whether or not they understand the code it verifies.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This directing has to remember the opening point that agents have different capabilities to humans.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A human wants to deal with less data. All abstractions we currently have (aggregation, visualizations etc) are designed to help humans deal with less data. An agent, however, needs more raw data; its strength lies in processing huge volumes of data at a rapid clip. Aggregation — the thing that makes a dashboard useful to a human — is often the thing that destroys the value of the signal for an agent</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Agents’ ability to consume lots of raw data also affects how we build systems. More logging gives agents more data to chew on.</p>

<p>And thus far, the human is still necessary to direct the agent</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In each of the three bugs chronicled in this post, the agent was more than capable of getting to the root cause by itself, but it didn’t do so. It didn’t set up a control in the “latency bug” until I asked it to</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While it’s a long post, it’s engagingly written and full of useful details. I hope my little summary encourages you to give it the read it deserves.</p>

---

### [When Software Meets Hardware](https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>When software meets hardware, engineering principles remain largely the
    same, but the sensible defaults often change. <b class="author">Kuldeep Singh</b>
    describes our experience modernizing a large IoT fleet of battery-powered
    sensors. Our familiar principles remained important; what changed was how we
    applied them. These practices emerged through experiments, failures, and
    operational experience&#x2014;each challenge helping us replace a reasonable
    assumption with a more sensible default. </p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html">more…</a></p>

      colours, fonts and layouts. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> covers
      the basics of color theory and how to build a reusable palette, the
      essentials of typography, including serif, sans-serif, handwritten and
      monospace fonts, plus the finer points of kerning and leading, and why a
      near-blank layout might serve you better than the prescribed layouts that
      ship with your slideware.</p>

      practitioners writing for each other forms a distinct style of writing. He
      now adds a section to the article with more detail on the craft of how we
      write in this style. How the writer shows up in the prose, its four
      principles, the role of story, how to sensibly use AI, three levels of
      editing, and clarity of the audience.</p>

### [Are Harnesses Worth the Cost?](https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/donkey-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p><b class="author">Naresh Jain</b> compares coding agents building the same
      API service with and without a behavioral harness. A frontier model, GPT-6
      Sol, passed every external evaluation either way. But a cost-optimized
      model, GPT-6 Luna, succeeded in only 3 of 10 unharnessed runs and all 10
      with the harness. While adding the harness increased token usage and
      runtime for Luna, the harnessed Luna runs achieved the same 10-for-10
      reliability as GPT-6 Sol while costing 33% less per run ($0.273 vs
      $0.406). This experiment suggests a behavioral harness might be a
      practical way to get dependable results from smaller, cost-optimized
      models.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

---

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>

### [Bliki: Sensible Default](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SensibleDefault.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A Sensible Default is a practice that, absent some overriding context,
  should be used when carrying out a certain kind of task. In software
  development such sensible defaults might include things like “use version
  control”, “separate UI logic from domain logic”, “automate deployment
  pipelines”.</p>

<p>The term “sensible default” is a deliberate contrast to “best practice”.
  Folks dislike calling things “best practice” because that term implies a
  general presumption that the best practice is something we should always
  expect to do. A “sensible default”, however, is something that should be
  reassessed in a new context, something that can (and should) be overridden
  when circumstances change.</p>

<p>I first heard the term when it was popularized within Thoughtworks by Evan
  Bottcher. He got the name from talking to James Ross, and found the phrasing
  appealing as carried the nuance he was seeking, a known-good starting
  point.</p>

<blockquote>
<p>A sensible default is what we'd expect you to do, the practices to apply,
    if there are no hard constraints in the environment. Do these practices, or
    do better, and be prepared to explain why you've chosen some other way.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- Evan Bottcher</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Thoughtworks has since made much of this concept, including <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/topic/sensible-defaults">publishing
  a playbook</a> of the ones we use. We expect people to be familiar with these
  defaults, ready to use them when starting any new piece of work. They are our
  defaults because we've used them in many situations and found them to be
  effective. But teams should also be familiar with their limitations, and able
  to judge whether they should be changed depending on the particular
  circumstances. As the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">twelfth agile principle</a>
  says: “At regular intervals, the team reflects on how to become more
  effective, then tunes and adjusts its behavior accordingly.” We also reassess
  these defaults regularly - this is the heart of the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">Thoughtworks Technology
  Radar</a>.</p>

<p class="p-sub">Searching on the web led me to <a href="https://stevebennett.co/posts/the-power-of-sensible-defaults">a post
  from Steve Bennett on Sensible Defaults</a>. The post was written at about the
  time Evan talked to James. I do not know whether it was where James got the
  name from, or whether it appeared in parallel.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/24/harder/">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The more time I spend working with coding agents, the more convinced I am that they make software engineering even harder</p>

  <p>We can do amazing things with them, but unlocking their full potential requires extraordinary discipline and knowledge</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This has been a constant impression I get from following Willison’s writing. While things like <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html">vibe coding</a> get a lot of attention, the real strength of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a> relies on more sophisticated techniques - and these are not easy to learn or execute. It’s a reason I’m wary of extrapolating my own dabblings into firm opinions about how to use the genie.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Harper Reed explored what turns agents into hackers by <a href="https://harper.blog/2026/09/22/break-away/">creating a breakaway agent</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It ran and ran attacking all the machines on the same subnet, and was very effective. It didn’t really get very far, but it exhausted a lot of options, and was pretty fun to watch. (Just a reminder that this was on my local network with local boxes - don’t do this on a hosted box. That would be very rude.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The interesting observation from this was that a core enabler for this was “unlimited tokens”. He usually doesn’t see agents trying to do stuff like this because there’s a limit on how many turns they can take. For this experiment he gave them unlimited tokens by using an open weight model.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This type of experience must be part of a lot of these LLMs training. They are very effective at attacking these types of problems. They don’t give up once it appears impossible, they just keep trying to figure out how to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This echoes Nate Silver’s observation that the striking capability of these models is that they not that they are super-intelligent - but they are <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">super-persistent</a>. Which is especially worrying when we are <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">wiring them into everything</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Which all makes me think that when it comes to AI and LLMs: <strong>why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?</strong></p>

<p>After all, the AI labs trained them to be super-persistent, why did they not train them to be well-behaved? It’s as if a human trains a dog to bite children, and we blame the dog rather than the trainer.</p>

<p>A decade ago, a dog ran in front of me while I was riding my bike, putting me into the hospital with a broken arm and a broken face. Massachusetts law makes owners <em>strictly liable</em> for what their dogs do. That meant I didn’t have to prove the owners were negligent in how they controlled their dog or fenced their property - they had to pay my hospital expenses (in practice their insurance company paid my insurance company). There should be something along these lines for LLMs. Those that train the LLM should be responsible for what it does, after all if it has such a galaxy brain it should be able to tell if it’s doing something wrong and either stop or get a human’s explicit approval.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davis has <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/13-theses-on-agentic-ai-and-regulation">13 theses on agentic AI and regulation</a>. These include:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The AI industry also seems to be quite committed to the idea that nonaligned computer-hacking behaviour in agent swarms is in some way an emergent property of the LLMs, arising from their general intelligence (and therefore inextricable from the general project of improving them). I don’t think this is necessarily the case at all – the fact that the internal message logs produced by the LLMs in things like the Huggingface attack seem to completely reproduce the prose style of hacker chat logs compiled from “capture the flag” competitions suggests to me that it’s more likely to be learned behaviour from specific parts of the training material.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The fact that anyone with cash to spare can buy the right to send queries to a frontier LLM is a policy choice, not a fact of nature.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If any frontier lab tries to claim that they can’t publish anything for safety reasons, they are giving the game away that they actually believe that they have significantly more control over the model’s behaviour than they are pretending to have.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There seems a common view that making agents safer means we have to slow down their development. But why is improving their safety not a form of progress? I say we don’t slow down the development of LLMs, but we redirect their education into being more civil members of society.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The idea that LLMs make junior professionals less valuable is a common one - although I’m seeing plenty of contrary activity, with some organizations understanding that training the future professional in the context of LLMs may be even more urgent. We need people who know how to utilize AI to do professional work effectively. Recent graduates, who are growing up with LLMs, are often well-suited to figuring this future out.</p>

<p>When thinking of junior professionals one of their values is often missed. Juniors are often valuable because they need to be taught by senior professionals - and that coaching is an important part of the development of a senior professional. I’ve always found that teaching a topic is one of the most valuable tools for me to gain a greater understanding of that topic. I don’t really know something until I have to explain it.</p>

<!-- 
 ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄


In the software world, we've talked a fair bit about Cognitive Debt, the concern that agents can write code faster than we humans can understand it. It strikes me that this phenomenon is occurring with AI in general. AI tooling is advancing faster than we can understand how to live with it. Part of me thinks this is a reason TODO FINISH


<!-- 
Cognitive Debt mentioned in phschology since at least 2016? see <https://suttonpsychology.blogspot.com/2025/07/cognitive-debt-overview.html#google_vignette>

Appeaed more closely with AI after MIT media lab paper <https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai></https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai> <https://www.brainonllm.com> this was in Summer 2025

Neither of these is quite like the idea of not understanding a codebase


-->

### [Fragments: September 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Rob Bowley is “flipping tables in his head” with <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">anger at the current media coverage of the danger of AI killing us all</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The risk I’m worried about isn’t a future machine deciding to wipe us out. It’s today’s AI, being wired into everything, carelessly and fast.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Cyber attacks have already cost millions of dollars in lost economic output, often without AI being involved at all. Bowley feels the push to slow down AI is distracting us from the problems that are lurking with current technology. Too often agents are deployed in situations where they include the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">Lethal Trifecta</a>, opening up a gaping security hole.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What we really need to slow down on is wiring it all up to everything. Not because of what the models might become, but because nobody has worked out how to do this safely yet. We are building on something we don’t know how to contain, and shipping it to everyone while we work it out.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I ran into Nikita Prokopov’s post: <a href="https://tonsky.me/blog/syntax-highlighting/">I am sorry, but everyone is getting syntax highlighting wrong</a>. His core complaint is about color themes that give every different code element a unique color.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>if everything is highlighted, nothing stands out. Your eye adapts and considers it a new norm: everything is bright and shiny, and instead of getting separated, it all blends together.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He recommends using an absolute minimum of colors, in his case four: string, constants, comments, and top-level definitions. That’s not far off my approach, where I’m also careful to use muted colors for things that shouldn’t stand out, and bright colors for things that should (primarily function names when they are defined).</p>

<p>It’s common for color schemes to mute comments so they are easily skipped. He agrees that this good when there is excessive commenting, but when comments are used properly they are important so need bright highlighting. I also like his suggestion to use background colors for light mode work. I use light mode, and that’s a tip I should try out.</p>

<p>With agentic engineering, lots of folks are reading more code than ever. Careful use of color can do much to make that easier.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Like many folks whose remaining hair is getting gray, I’ve been rolling my eyes about all this talk about Forward Deployed Engineers, as much of it involves breathlessly relating what so many of us have been advocating for decades. I did find this recent <a href="https://www.latent.space/p/forward-deployed-engineer-best-practices">post by Vinoo Ganesh</a> interesting, as he’s deep in this trend, including a chunk of time at Palantir, who may be patient zero for FDEs.</p>

<p>If you know me at all, you’ll not be surprised by my lack of surprise at this observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A few months ago, a16z launched the Forward Deployed Engineer Fellowship and I was nominated as one of the fellows, alongside a handful of people I used to work with. It’s a great program and I’ve enjoyed so many of the conversations. Last week I went to my first fellow dinner in SF.</p>

  <p>Around the table were FDEs from Snowflake, Anthropic, and a number of startups I’d been reading about, and over the course of the evening it became clear that we were all using the same two words (forward deployed) to describe jobs that had almost nothing in common. In one part of the conversation an FDE was a sales engineer who joined ‘the second call,’ somewhere else it was a quota-carrying rep who could write Python, and a few seats down it was closer to a consultant with a laptop and a statement of work, brought in to deliver something the product couldn’t.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Ganesh goes on to explain his view of what an FDE should do, and it’s all sensible stuff (albeit written with rather more LLM-voice than I would prefer). He says the FDEs job is to understand the business, to “collect nouns and verbs”, which mirrors what the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain-Driven Design</a> folks have been doing since before Eric wrote the blue book.</p>

<p>Despite all my eyeball rolling at this, the FDE meme is pushing for something valuable. Yes, it’s easy for me to remark that it’s nothing more than the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">Agile Manifesto’s principle</a> that “Business people and developers must work
together daily throughout the project”, or the desire to co-locate users and developers which my colleagues have been championing for all of this century. I’ve argued for decades that the biggest issue in software development is the communication between developers and the folks that benefit from software, and thus we need to focus on bridging the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/videos.html#y2007-crevasse">yawning crevasse of doom</a>. But despite all this, we haven’t had much success, so I think it’s important that a new generation of pundits try again, with some different framing, names, and slogans.</p>

<p>Ganesh’s perspective is not a custom software developer’s point of view, but rather a product - or more strictly - platform team’s. The FDE is a developer who “sits with” their users, applies customizations - but importantly - feeds these changes back to the core platform to decide whether the platform should be enhanced for everyone else.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Keeping the customer happy is a real job and a good one. It belongs to solutions architects, who are rightly measured on it. The forward deployed engineer is there to turn what the field teaches into the thing every future customer gets. An FDE engagement that ends with one delighted account and nothing changed upstream has failed at the only thing the role exists for. You got the context and you spent it locally.</p>
</blockquote>

### [Beyond API Compatibility: Building a Tool to Measure the Blast Radius of Library Changes](https://netflixtechblog.com/beyond-api-compatibility-building-a-tool-to-measure-the-blast-radius-of-library-changes-9474f2fd4f90?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <p>By <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/robertoperezalcolea/">Roberto Perez Alcolea</a>, Java Platform Team</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*K06WZmnRhhXBboIskWb8qg.png" /><figcaption>Quasar connects a proposed API change with observed references in consumer repositories, giving the producer evidence for a release decision</figcaption></figure><p>At Netflix, shared Java libraries support applications developed across a large, independently evolving repository ecosystem. A library change begins in one repository, but its impact can reach many others. Producers can see the API diff in front of them. What they often cannot see is who still depends on the symbols that changed.</p><p>The need for that visibility grows as coding agents take on more of the software development lifecycle. An agent can accelerate a local edit, but without cross-repository context it cannot judge downstream risk or help plan consumer migrations. The evidence a producer uses to assess a change can also guide the agent working on it.</p><p>Consider a common case: removing a deprecated API. Deprecated entry points expand the surface producers must test, document, and support, and they can constrain how a library evolves.</p><p>Suppose a replacement has been available for several releases and the old method cannot express options required by new behavior. The library’s tests pass, and a compatibility checker flags the removal. The producer still cannot answer the questions that determine what to do next:</p><ul><li>Does any consumer still call this method?</li><li>Are consumers concentrated on one version or spread across several generations?</li><li>Does another library implement or wrap the API, multiplying its impact?</li><li>Is direct coordination sufficient, or does this require a deprecation and migration campaign?</li></ul><p>At small scale, producers can ask nearby teams or search a handful of repositories. Across many repositories, it is harder to find every caller of a method and the library version each one uses. Those answers are scattered across consumer codebases and builds.</p><p>Without that evidence, a producer may release a breaking change and discover the impact through consumer failures, or preserve an old API indefinitely because the risk is unknown. Libraries need to evolve, and their producers need evidence that helps them do so intentionally.</p><p>We built <strong>Quasar</strong> to index symbol definitions and references across Netflix repositories. For library producers, that shared index helps answer two questions:</p><ol><li><strong>What changed in the library’s public API?</strong></li><li><strong>Which repositories use the exact symbols that changed?</strong></li></ol><p>The first identifies a potentially incompatible change. The second estimates its observed blast radius. Together, they turn a generic compatibility warning into evidence a producer can act on before merging or releasing.</p><h3>From API Changes to Consumer Impact</h3><p>Existing techniques each see a different part of the problem. Binary and source compatibility tools identify contract changes, but cannot tell who uses the changed API. Dependency graphs show declared reach, but cannot prove that code calls a particular method. Text search may miss overloads, inheritance, or the artifact version behind a call. Consumer builds provide strong validation, but running them across the fleet is expensive.</p><p>Quasar builds on <a href="https://github.com/sourcegraph/scip">SCIP</a>, an open interchange format for semantic code intelligence. Language-specific indexers encode definitions, references, and relationships using stable symbol identifiers. Quasar retains API definitions for library versions and commits alongside references from consumer repositories. That shared identity lets it connect an API change in one repository to exact usages elsewhere, even when those repositories were indexed independently.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*5c7ImyYuNCfO7Tv_ma0EYg.png" /></figure><p>On each enrolled CI build, language-specific indexers submit SCIP indexes to Quasar’s ingestion service. It writes API definitions and consumer references as Parquet in Amazon S3, retaining the symbol identity, dependency version, and source location needed to investigate a change.</p><p>During pull-request review, Quasar compares indexed base and head definitions and counts direct consumer references with Amazon Athena. Quasar exposes those findings through its REST and GraphQL APIs and MCP server; follow-up usage queries provide dependency-version breakdowns and source locations. A separate compatibility-impact assessment can also follow implementations and their callers when a producer supplies a changed JVM symbol.</p><p>Consider this simplified API change:</p><pre>public interface Client {<br />- Result getLegacyValue(Request request);<br />+ Result getValue(Request request, Options options);<br />}</pre><p>For a detailed compatibility-impact assessment of this removal, a producer-side tool supplies the old method descriptor. Quasar constructs its canonical symbol identity. This abridged excerpt from the direct-reference portion of Quasar’s Amazon Athena query shows the essential match:</p><pre>SELECT c.change_id, 'DIRECT_REFERENCE' AS evidence_kind,<br />       r.scm_type, r.org, r.repo, r.dep_version, r.file_path<br />FROM requested_changes c<br />JOIN quasar.symbol_refs r<br />  ON r.symbol_canonical = c.target_canonical</pre><p>requested_changes contains the API changes submitted by the producer. The SQL excerpt finds repositories that call the removed method. Quasar also checks implementations and calls through them, so it can count each affected repository once. It returns sample dependency versions and file locations to help investigate the result.</p><p>For the Client change, this tells the producer which repositories still use getLegacyValue, which versions they use, and where some of those calls occur. That helps the producer decide whether to coordinate a migration before removing the method.</p><h3>Bringing the Analysis Before Release</h3><p>Version-to-version comparison is valuable when planning an upgrade or writing a migration guide. Producers benefit even more when feedback arrives during code review.</p><p>For pull-request analysis, Quasar stores API definition snapshots keyed by commit. Given a base and head commit, it classifies symbols as added, removed, or signature-modified. Removals and modifications then become candidates for consumer-impact analysis.</p><p>An incompatible change is not automatically a high-risk change. Quasar reports the observed impact so the producer can decide what to do; it does not enforce a release policy. “No references observed” means only that Quasar found none in the indexed population. Reflection, configuration, dynamic loading, stale branches, or incomplete indexing can hide consumers. The result needs to show whether the requested commits were indexed, how fresh the consumer indexes are, and whether evidence was truncated.</p><p>Pull-request review requires indexed snapshots for the exact base and head commits. If either is unavailable, Quasar reports that it cannot compare them; it does not substitute a default-branch snapshot. This keeps a missing comparison from looking like a clean API diff.</p><p>Return to our Client change. This illustrative report brings together signals available through Quasar's API comparison, consumer-usage queries, and library-level criticality. It is not a literal response from one API:</p><pre>{<br />  &quot;change&quot;: &quot;REMOVED&quot;,<br />  &quot;symbol&quot;: &quot;Client#getLegacyValue(Request)&quot;,<br />  &quot;affectedRepositories&quot;: 137,<br />  &quot;versionDistribution&quot;: [<br />    { &quot;version&quot;: &quot;4.2.1&quot;, &quot;repositories&quot;: 86 },<br />    { &quot;version&quot;: &quot;4.1.7&quot;, &quot;repositories&quot;: 38 },<br />    { &quot;version&quot;: &quot;3.9.4&quot;, &quot;repositories&quot;: 13 }<br />  ],<br />  &quot;sampleConsumerLocations&quot;: [<br />    {<br />      &quot;repository&quot;: &quot;checkout-service&quot;,<br />      &quot;filePath&quot;: &quot;src/main/java/com/example/checkout/CheckoutClient.java&quot;,<br />      &quot;lines&quot;: [84, 129]<br />    },<br />    {<br />      &quot;repository&quot;: &quot;client-wrapper&quot;,<br />      &quot;filePath&quot;: &quot;src/main/java/com/example/client/ClientAdapter.java&quot;,<br />      &quot;lines&quot;: [57]<br />    }<br />  ],<br />  &quot;libraryCriticality&quot;: {<br />    &quot;tier&quot;: 1,<br />    &quot;basis&quot;: &quot;highest-tier downstream application&quot;<br />  },<br />  &quot;comparison&quot;: {<br />    &quot;baseShaFound&quot;: true,<br />    &quot;headShaFound&quot;: true<br />  }<br />}</pre><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*QLVdKPyYkqJcNgt0rVN39Q.png" /><figcaption><em>An impact report shows 137 affected repositories distributed across library versions 4.2.1, 4.1.7, and 3.9.4.</em></figcaption></figure><p>The repository count is symbol-specific: in this example, 137 indexed repositories refer to the removed method. The version distribution reveals a dominant cohort on 4.2.1 and a smaller long tail still on 3.9.4. A producer can inspect affected repositories and retrieve indexed file paths, with line numbers where the index provides them. The JSON shows only two sample locations to keep the example readable. The filePath and lines shape mirrors Quasar's query contract. The tier is broader context about the library, inherited from its highest-criticality downstream application; it does not imply that all 137 callers are Tier 1.</p><p>Together, those signals change the producer’s plan. Instead of merging the removal and waiting for failures, the producer can retain and deprecate the old method, publish a migration path, coordinate a broader migration, and track the long-tail versions before removal. File paths and available line numbers turn that scope into a concrete work list for engineers or coding agents. The same API diff paired with weak or stale evidence would lead to a different action: refresh the indexes before deciding. The value is not merely detecting the removal; it is choosing a proportionate next step.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*o08l-rlQ0D2oo1fBpH5ICw.png" /><figcaption>A pre-release workflow enriches API changes with consumer impact and evidence quality before the producer decides how to proceed</figcaption></figure><h3>A Polyglot Foundation with SCIP</h3><p>The shared <a href="https://github.com/sourcegraph/scip">SCIP</a> format also lets Quasar index several language ecosystems. Each language indexer resolves its own code, while Quasar can use the resulting symbol identities to find references across repositories.</p><p>Conceptually, client-lib indexes Client#getLegacyValue(Request) as a definition, while checkout-service indexes a reference to the same symbol. Quasar can then connect the library API to its consumer across repositories.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*RMR331Wf7c4Xx3GTD5_7Rw.png" /><figcaption><em>Language-specific indexers emit SCIP definitions, references, and relationships into Quasar’s shared semantic graph.</em></figcaption></figure><p>Relationships are especially useful when generated code separates the API a producer owns from the symbols consumers invoke. For example, Quasar can trace a Protobuf definition through its generated Java members to the repositories that use them, even when consumer code never names the .proto element.</p><p>SCIP records symbols and their references; it does not determine whether an API change violates an ecosystem’s compatibility rules. Today, Quasar compares indexed JVM and Protobuf API definitions and traces Protobuf changes through generated Java to find affected repositories. A separate JVM compatibility-impact path uses breaks identified by a producer-side tool to query affected repositories. Go, Python, JavaScript/TypeScript, and Rust support usage queries, but Quasar does not yet assess their breaking API changes.</p><h3>Moving Risk Assessment Earlier</h3><p>Today, Quasar surfaces API-change findings on pull requests, including observed consumer counts and confidence. Producers can use its other queries to inspect dependency versions and source locations, with library criticality as broader context. Together, those signals support a decision before merge.</p><p>We want to move that context earlier. An agent writing library code should recognize when it is changing a public symbol and consult Quasar before the change becomes a pull request. Quasar’s APIs and <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/">Model Context Protocol</a> (MCP) integration provide an access path, but access alone does not create awareness. The coding workflow must also recognize public API edits so the agent knows when to query impact and when to involve the producer.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*3VqsUQ4rQ3tl5VUPw9ESUQ.png" /><figcaption><em>Today Quasar assesses risk on a pull request. The future workflow gives agents consumer-impact context while they write code, before the pull request is opened.</em></figcaption></figure><p>Earlier guidance will not replace pull-request analysis. The PR assessment will still verify the concrete diff and catch changes that were not recognized during coding. Coverage, freshness, ambiguity, unsupported platforms, and truncation must travel through both checks in machine-readable form. An agent should not turn “no observed usages in the available index” into “safe to remove.”</p><p>Today, agents can query Quasar through MCP when prompted. We are working toward making that context part of the coding workflow, then using identified call sites to prepare migration changes. Quasar supplies semantic evidence, an agent reduces migration toil, and the library producer remains responsible for API and release decisions.</p><h3>What We Learned</h3><p>Building this workflow led us to four practical lessons.</p><ul><li><strong>Separate compatibility from reach.</strong> A removed method can break the API contract even when Quasar finds no callers. A list of affected repositories and their library versions shows the migration work ahead. Producers need both signals to choose a release path.</li><li><strong>Make the evidence inspectable.</strong> A repository count is more useful with sample call sites and a version breakdown. The result also needs to show which commits were indexed, how fresh the consumer indexes are, and whether evidence was truncated. Otherwise, “no usage found” can be mistaken for proof that nobody is affected.</li><li><strong>Keep usage and compatibility separate across languages.</strong> SCIP lets Quasar find symbol references in several ecosystems. Deciding whether an API change is breaking requires compatibility rules or a trusted producer-side signal for that ecosystem.</li><li><strong>Bring evidence to the producer while choices are open.</strong> Pull-request findings can prompt a producer to retain an API, publish a migration path, or coordinate consumers before merge. Moving the same evidence into the coding workflow could shape the edit earlier. After release, it still helps locate consumers that need to migrate.</li></ul><p>Today, Quasar indexes JVM, Go, Python, JavaScript/TypeScript, and Rust code for cross-repository usage analysis. It compares JVM APIs and traces Protobuf changes through generated Java. Producers can review change findings through Quasar’s REST and GraphQL APIs or its MCP server, then investigate affected repositories and call sites. We are extending this foundation toward more ecosystem-specific compatibility checks, deeper code-review integration, and agent-assisted consumer migrations that keep release approval with the producer.</p><p>Quasar is internal to Netflix, but the pattern is portable: index code semantically, retain API definitions, compare them at the point of change, query affected consumers, and report the quality of the evidence. Library producers need more than a compatibility verdict. They need to know who may be affected and have enough time to act before release.</p><p>A huge thank you to the Java Platform team at Netflix. Their expertise, collaboration, and support made this work possible</p><h3>Related Talks</h3><ul><li><a href="https://dpe.org/sessions/roberto-perez-alcolea-aubrey-chipman/from-lag-to-lightning-transforming-dependency-update-timelines/">From Lag to Lightning: Transforming Dependency Update Timelines</a> explores how Netflix shortened dependency-update timelines.</li><li><a href="https://moderne.ai/code-remix-sessions/netflix-journey-automated-code-changes-crs-2026">Netflix’s Journey to Confident Automated Code Changes</a> discusses building confidence in automated changes at scale.</li></ul><img alt="" height="1" src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&amp;referrerSource=full_rss&amp;postId=9474f2fd4f90" width="1" /><hr /><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/beyond-api-compatibility-building-a-tool-to-measure-the-blast-radius-of-library-changes-9474f2fd4f90">Beyond API Compatibility: Building a Tool to Measure the Blast Radius of Library Changes</a> was originally published in <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com">Netflix TechBlog</a> on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.</p>

### [LLMs are making formal software verification feasible at a much larger scale](https://www.anthropic.com/research/formal-verification)

**分数**: 440 | [讨论](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50202217)
